Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.


-----Examples-----
Input
abc
cbaabc

Output
2
3 1
1 3

Input
aaabrytaaa
ayrat

Output
3
1 1
6 5
8 7

Input
ami
no

Output
-1



-----Note-----

In the first sample string "cbaabc" = "cba" + "abc".

In the second sample: "ayrat" = "a" + "yr" + "at".

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of string `s` and want to build target string `t`
2. From each copy of `s`, we can:
   - Cut out a continuous substring (from position x to y)
   - Optionally reverse it (if x > y)
   - Add it to the end of our track
3. We need to find the minimum number of such pieces to get `t`

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains a substring that doesn't appear in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to greedily build `t` from left to right by finding substrings of `s` that match the remaining part of `t`
3. For each matching substring, we need to determine if it should be used in original or reversed order

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Check if solution exists
We need to verify that every substring of `t` that we want to match actually exists in `s` (in both forward and backward forms).

### Step 2: Greedy approach
- Process `t` from left to right
- For each position, find the longest suffix of the remaining string that appears in `s`
- If we can't find a match, return -1
- Record the start and end positions of the matched substring

### Step 3: Handle reversal
- If the substring appears in `s` but we need it reversed, we use reversed form
- We need to check both forward and backward existence

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = len(t)
    if n == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Check if t can be constructed
    # For each position, we need to verify that the remaining part of t
    # can be formed from substrings of s
    
    result = []
    remaining = t
    pos = 0
    
    while pos < len(remaining):
        # Find the longest prefix of remaining that appears in s (forward or backward)
        found = False
        
        # Try to find a match in s (both forward and backward)
        for length in range(1, len(s) + 1):
            if pos + length > len(remaining):
                break
                
            substr = remaining[pos:pos + length]
            
            # Check if substr appears in s (forward)
            if substr in s:
                # We found a forward match
                # Find where it appears in s
                idx = s.find(substr)
                if idx != -1:
                    # We can use this in original order
                    result.append((idx + 1, idx + length))  # 1-indexed
                    pos += length
                    found = True
                    break
                    
            # Check if reverse of substr appears in s
            rev_substr = substr[::-1]
            if rev_substr in s:
                # We found a reverse match
                idx = s.find(rev_substr)
                if idx != -1:
                    # We can use this in reversed order
                    result.append((idx + 1, idx + length))  # 1-indexed, but since it's reversed, we need to adjust
                    # Actually, we need to find the actual positions
                    temp_idx = s.find(rev_substr)
                    # The original substring is reversed, so we need to flip the indices
                    # If we take substring from temp_idx to temp_idx + length in reverse
                    result.append((temp_idx + 1, temp_idx + length))
                    pos += length
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if not found:
            # No match found
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Validate that our construction is actually correct
    # Build the constructed string and verify it matches t
    constructed = ""
    for x, y in result:
        if x <= y:
            constructed += s[x-1:y]
        else:
            constructed += s[x-1:y][::-1]
    
    if constructed != t:
        print(-1)
        return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a string `s` (the coating available in the shop)
2. We can cut out any continuous substring from `s` and either use it as-is or reversed
3. We need to build target string `t` by concatenating these substrings
4. We want the minimum number of such substrings
5. If impossible, return -1

## Key Insights

1. The problem is asking: can we build string `t` using substrings from `s`, possibly reversed?
2. For each position in `t`, we need to find a substring from `s` that matches either `t[i:i+len]` or its reverse.
3. We want to minimize the total number of substrings used.
4. This can be thought of as a dynamic programming problem where we build `t` character by character.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if solution is possible**: For the solution to exist, every substring we use must be found in `s` or its reverse. But this is always true if we can find a match.

2. **Greedy approach**: For each position, try to extend the current match as long as possible using substrings from `s`.

3. **DP approach**: Let `dp[i]` be the minimum number of substrings to form `t[0:i]`.

4. **For each position**, try all possible substring lengths from 1 to `len(t)-i` and check if the substring or its reverse matches `t[i:i+length]` or some substring from `s`.

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem is simpler than I initially thought.

Actually, we can use a greedy approach:
- For each position in `t`, try to match as many characters as possible with a substring from `s` (either original or reversed)
- We want to minimize the total number of pieces

But actually, we can use a simpler approach: for each prefix of `t`, check if we can extend it using a substring from `s`.

## Algorithm

1. For each position in `t`, try to find the longest prefix of `t` that can be formed using substrings from `s` (in original or reversed form)
2. Use DP where `dp[i]` = minimum pieces to form `t[0:i]`
3. For each position, try all possible substring lengths

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = len(t)
    m = len(s)
    
    # Check if it's possible to form t
    # For every substring in t, it must be equal to some substring of s or its reverse
    
    # dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to form t[0:i]
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] stores the last piece that formed t[0:i]
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    
    # For each position in t
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible lengths of the last piece
        for length in range(1, i + 1):
            # Check if t[i-length:i] can be formed by a substring of s or its reverse
            substr = t[i-length:i]
            
            # Check if substr is a substring of s
            if substr in s:
                dp[i] = min(dp[i], dp[i-length] + 1)
                parent[i] = (i-length, i-1, length)  # start, end, length
            
            # Check if reverse of substr is a substring of s
            rev_sub = substr[::-1]
            if rev_sub in s:
                dp[i] = min(dp[i], dp[i-length] + 1)
                parent[i] = (i-length, i-1, length)  # store that we used reverse
                
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Reconstruct the solution
    result = []
    pos = n
    while pos > 0:
        length = 0
        start = 0
        # Find the last piece
        if parent[pos] != -1:
            start, end, length = parent[pos]
            piece_start = start
            piece_end = end
            
            # Determine if it's original or reversed
            substr = t[piece_start:piece_end+1]
            rev_sub = substr[::-1]
            
            # Check which one was actually used
            if substr in s:
                # Original
                result.append((piece_start + 1, piece_end + 1))
            else:
                # Reverse
                result.append((piece_start + 1, piece_end + 1))
                
            pos = start
    
    # Reverse the result to get correct order
    result.reverse()
    
    print(dp[n])
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)

solve()
```

Wait, I see issues in my reasoning. Let me fix this.

The problem is simpler: we need to find a sequence of substrings from `s` (original or reversed) that concatenate to `t`.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = len(t)
    m = len(s)
    
    # dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to form t[0:i]
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] stores (start, end, original_reversed) for the last piece
    parent = [None] * (n + 1)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible last pieces
        for j in range(i):
            # Try to use a piece from j to i-1
            piece = t[j:i]
            
            # Check if piece is in s (original or reversed)
            if piece in s:
                if dp[j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (j, i-1, False)  # original
            
            # Check if reverse of piece is in s
            rev_piece = piece[::-1]
            if rev_piece in s:
                if dp[j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (j, i-1, True)  # reversed
    
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Reconstruct the solution
    pieces = []
    pos = n
    while pos > 0:
        j, k, is_reversed = parent[pos]
        start, end = j, k
        
        # Convert to 1-indexed
        start += 1
        end += 1
        
        if is_reversed:
            pieces.append((start, end))
        else:
            pieces.append((start, end))
            
        pos = j
    
    pieces.reverse()
    
    print(dp[n])
    for start, end in pieces:
        print(start, end)

solve()
```

Actually, I need to check if the substring actually appears in `s`.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = len(t)
    
    # dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to form t[0:i]
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] = (prev_pos, start_idx, end_idx, is_reversed)
    parent = {}
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible previous positions
        for j in range(i):
            piece = t[j:i]
            
            # Check if piece appears in s (original)
            if piece in s:
                if dp[j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (j, j, i-1, False)  # prev_pos, start, end, is_reversed
            
            # Check if reverse of piece appears in s
            rev_piece = piece[::-1]
            if rev_piece in s:
                if dp[j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (j, j, i-1, True)
    
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Reconstruct
    result = []
    pos = n
    while pos > 0:
        j, start, end, is_reversed = parent[pos]
        start_idx = start + 1
        end_idx = end + 1
        
        result.append((start_idx, end_idx))
        
        pos = j
    
    result.reverse()
    
    print(dp[n])
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)
```

def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = len(t)
    
    # dp[i] = minimum number of pieces to form t[0:i]
    dp = [float('inf')] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 0
    
    # parent[i] = (prev_pos, start_idx, end_idx, is_reversed)
    parent = {}
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        # Try all possible previous positions
        for j in range(i):
            piece = t[j:i]
            
            # Check if piece appears in s (original)
            if piece in s:
                if dp[j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (j, j, i-1, False)  # prev_pos, start, end, is_reversed
            
            # Check if reverse of piece appears in s
            rev_piece = piece[::-1]
            if rev_piece in s:
                if dp[j] + 1 < dp[i]:
                    dp[i] = dp[j] + 1
                    parent[i] = (j, j, i-1, True)
    
    if dp[n] == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Reconstruct
    result = []
    pos = n
    while pos > 0:
        j, start, end, is_reversed = parent[pos]
        start_idx = start + 1
        end_idx = end + 1
        
        result.append((start_idx, end_idx))
        
        pos = j
    
    result.reverse()
    
    print(dp[n])
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a coating string `s`
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` (continuous piece) and glue it to the end of our target string `t`
3. We can choose to flip (reverse) the substring before gluing
4. We need to find the minimum number of such operations to create string `t`

## Key Insights

1. If we can't create `t` from `s`, return -1
2. We need to find substrings of `s` that can form the missing parts of `t`
3. For each position in `t`, we need to determine which substring from `s` we can use to match it
4. We can use dynamic programming or greedy approach

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if it's possible**: If `t` contains any character not present in `s`, it's impossible
2. **Greedy matching**: For each segment of `t`, try to match it with a substring from `s`
3. **Handle reversal**: For each segment, check if we can get the required pattern by using original or reversed substring

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if it's possible to create t from s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Find all possible substrings of s
    # Since we're looking for minimum number of pieces, we can use greedy approach
    # For each position in t, find the shortest substring from s that matches
    
    result = []
    i = 0  # current position in t
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to match current segment with some substring from s
        found = False
        
        # Check all possible substrings of s
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                substring = s[start:end+1]
                reversed_substring = substring[::-1]
                
                # Check if this substring or its reverse matches the remaining part of t
                remaining_len = len(t) - i
                if len(substring) <= remaining_len and len(reversed_substring) <= remaining_len:
                    # Check if substring matches part of t
                    if substring == t[i:i+len(substring)]:
                        result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed
                        i += len(substring)
                        found = True
                        break
                    # Check if reversed substring matches part of t
                    if reversed_substring == t[i:i+len(reversed_substring)]:
                        result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed
                        i += len(reversed_substring)
                        found = True
                        break
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
```
    
```python
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    result = []
```
```python
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to find the longest possible segment starting at position i
        max_length = 0
        best_segment = ""
        
        # Try lengths from 1 to len(t) - i
        for length in range(1, len(t) - i + 1):
            segment = t[i:i + length]
            
            # Check if segment exists in s (original) or reversed
            if segment in s or segment[::-1] in s:
                # This segment is valid
                if length > max_length:
                    max_length = length
                    best_segment = segment
        
        if max_length == 0:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Found a valid segment of max_length
        segment = best_segment
        segment_start = i
        segment_end = i + max_length - 1
        
        # Find where this segment appears in s (original or reversed)
        if segment in s:
            pos = s.find(segment)
            # Use original: (pos+1, pos+length)
            result.append((pos + 1, pos + max_length))
        else:
            # segment not in s, so reverse is in s
            rev_segment = segment[::-1]
            pos = s.find(rev_segment)
            # Use reversed: (pos+1, pos+max_length) with x > y
            result.append((pos + 1, pos + max_length))
        
        i += max_length
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of string `s` (the coating available in shop)
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reversed order
3. We want to build string `t` by concatenating these substrings
4. We need to find the minimum number of such substrings needed

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains any character not present in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We can use dynamic programming or greedy approach to build `t` from left to right
3. For each position in `t`, we need to find a substring from `s` that can match the remaining part of `t`
4. We have two options for each substring: original order or reversed order

## Algorithm Approach

1. First check if all characters in `t` are present in `s`. If not, return -1.
2. Use a greedy approach: try to match as much of `t` as possible with existing substrings from `s`.
3. For each position in `t`, find the longest substring from `s` that can match the remaining part of `t` in either original or reversed form.
4. If we can't match the entire string, return -1.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t are present in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Convert s to a list of all possible substrings (we don't need to store all, just check)
    # We'll use a more efficient approach: for each position in t, find the best match
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    pos = 0
    
    while pos < n:
        found = False
        
        # Try to find a substring from s that can match t[pos:]
        # We'll try all possible substrings of s
        best_len = 0
        best_start = -1
        best_end = -1
        best_match_start = -1
        best_match_end = -1
        
        # Try all possible substrings in s
        for i in range(len(s)):
            for j in range(i, len(s)):
                substring = s[i:j+1]
                reversed_substring = substring[::-1]
                
                # Check if substring matches t[pos:]
                if len(substring) <= n - pos:
                    if substring == t[pos:pos + len(substring)]:
                        if len(substring) > best_len:
                            best_len = len(substring)
                            best_start = i
                            best_end = j
                            best_match_start = pos
                            best_match_end = pos + len(substring) - 1
                
                # Check if reversed substring matches t[pos:]
                if len(reversed_substring) <= n - pos:
                    if reversed_substring == t[pos:pos + len(reversed_substring)]:
                        if len(reversed_substring) > best_len:
                            best_len = len(reversed_substring)
                            best_start = i
                            best_end = j
                            best_match_start = pos
                            best_match_end = pos + len(reversed_substring) - 1
        
        if best_len == 0:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Add this piece to result
        if best_match_start == pos and best_match_end == pos + best_len - 1:
            # Original order
            result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
            pos += best_len
        else:
            # Reversed order
            result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
            pos += best_len
    
    print(len(result))
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)

solve()
```

Wait, this approach is too slow for the constraints (strings up to 2100). Let me optimize it.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t are present in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    pos = 0
    
    while pos < n:
        # Try to find a substring from s that can match t[pos:]
        best_len = 0
        best_start = -1
        best_end = -1
        
        # Try all possible lengths from 1 to remaining length
        for length in range(1, n - pos + 1):
            # Check if we can find a substring of length 'length' that matches t[pos:pos+length]
            # or matches t[pos:pos+length] in reverse
            
            # Look for original match
            found_original = False
            for i in range(len(s) - length + 1):
                if s[i:i+length] == t[pos:pos+length]:
                    if length > best_len:
                        best_len = length
                        best_start = i
                        best_end = i + length - 1
            
            # Look for reversed match
            found_reversed = False
            for i in range(len(s) - length + 1):
                reversed_substring = s[i:i+length][::-1]
                if reversed_substring == t[pos:pos+length]:
                    if length > best_len:
                        best_len = length
                        best_start = i
                        best_end = i + length - 1
        
        if best_len == 0:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Add this piece to result
        result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
        pos += best_len
    
    print(len(result))
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)
```

This is still potentially slow. Let me reconsider. The worst case might be too high. Let me optimize further by precomputing all possible substrings and their reverses.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t are present in s
    s_chars = set(s)
    t_chars = set(t)
    
    if not t_chars.issubset(s_chars):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    pos = 0
    
    # Precompute all possible substrings and their reverses
    s_substrings = {}
    for i in range(len(s)):
        for j in range(i, len(s)):
            substring = s[i:j+1]
            reversed_substring = substring[::-1]
            if substring not in s_substrings:
                s_substrings[substring] = []
            if reversed_substring not in s_substrings:
                s_substrings[reversed_substring] = []
            s_substrings[substring].append((i, j))
            s_substrings[reversed_substring].append((i, j))
    
    while pos < n:
        best_len = 0
        best_start = -1
        best_end = -1
        
        # Try all possible lengths from 1 to remaining length
        for length in range(1, n - pos + 1):
            target = t[pos:pos+length]
            
            # Check if target exists in s as a substring
            if target in s_substrings:
                for start, end in s_substrings[target]:
                    if start >= 0 and end < len(s):
                        if length > best_len:
                            best_len = length
                            best_start = start
                            best_end = end
            
            # Check if target exists in s as a reversed substring
            reversed_target = target[::-1]
            if reversed_target in s_substrings:
                for start, end in s_substrings[reversed_target]:
                    if start >= 0 and end < len(s):
                        if length > best_len:
                            best_len = length
                            best_start = start
                            best_end = end
        
        if best_len == 0:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Add this piece to result
        result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
        pos += best_len
    
    print(len(result))
    for start, end in result:
        print(start, end)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.


-----Examples-----
Input
abc
cbaabc

Output
2
3 1
1 3

Input
aaabrytaaa
ayrat

Output
3
1 1
6 5
8 7

Input
ami
no

Output
-1



-----Note-----

In the first sample string "cbaabc" = "cba" + "abc".

In the second sample: "ayrat" = "a" + "yr" + "at".

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a string `s` (coatings)
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reversed order
3. We want to build string `t` by concatenating these substrings
4. We need to find the minimum number of such substrings needed

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains any character that doesn't appear in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We want to use as few pieces as possible, so we should try to use long substrings when possible
3. For each piece, we can either use substring `s[x:y+1]` or its reverse `s[x:y+1][::-1]`
4. We need to build `t` from left to right, matching characters with available substrings from `s`

## Approach

1. First check if all characters in `t` exist in `s` - if not, return -1
2. Use a greedy approach: try to match as much of `t` as possible with each piece from `s`
3. For each position in `t`, try to find a substring in `s` that can match the remaining suffix of `t`
4. Since we want minimum pieces, we should try to match long substrings first

## Detailed Algorithm

1. Check if all characters in `t` are present in `s`
2. Use dynamic programming or greedy matching to build `t`
3. For each position in `t`, find the longest possible substring in `s` that can match the remaining suffix of `t` (either directly or in reverse)

Actually, let me think of a simpler greedy approach:

1. If `t` contains any character not in `s`, return -1
2. Use a greedy algorithm that tries to match the longest possible substring at each step
3. For each position, try all possible substrings in `s` that can match the remaining part of `t` (either directly or reversed)

## Implementation Strategy

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Build the result
    result = []
    remaining = t
    
    while remaining:
        # Try to find the longest substring in s that matches remaining
        # Either directly or in reverse
        found = False
        
        # Try all possible substrings of s
        n = len(s)
        found_match = None
        
        # Try each possible ending position and length
        for end in range(n):
            # Try substrings ending at end position
            for start in range(end + 1):
                substr = s[start:end+1]
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Check if this substring matches the beginning of remaining
                if substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
                    
                if rev_substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed (reversed)
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Try to find a substring that matches a prefix of remaining
            # This should work since all chars exist in s
            # Try all possible substrings that can match any prefix
            for end in range(n):
                for start in range(end + 1):
                    substr = s[start:end+1]
                    rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                    
                    # Check if any prefix of remaining matches either substr or rev_substr
                    match_len = 0
                    for i in range(min(len(remaining), len(substr))):
                        if substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            match_len += 1
                        elif rev_substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            match_len += 1
                        else:
                            break
                    
                    if match_len > 0:
                        # We found a match, use it
                        if match_len == len(remaining):
                            result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                            remaining = ""
                            break
                        else:
                            # Need to match prefix of remaining
                            prefix = remaining[:match_len]
                            if prefix == substr:
                                result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                                remaining = remaining[match_len:]
                                break
                            if prefix == rev_substr:
                                result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                                remaining = remaining[match_len:]
                                break
                if remaining == "":
                    break
            if remaining == "":
                continue
                
        # If we haven't found a match after trying all possibilities, something's wrong
        # But we should always be able to match since all chars are in s
        if remaining != "":
            # Fallback: try to find any match
            for end in range(n):
                for start in range(end + 1):
                    substr = s[start:end+1]
                    rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                    
                    # Try to match prefix of remaining
                    max_match = 0
                    for i in range(min(len(remaining), len(substr))):
                        if substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            max_match += 1
                        elif rev_substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            max_match += 1
                        else:
                            break
                    
                    if max_match > 0:
                        # Use the first match we find
                        result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                        remaining = remaining[max_match:]
                        break
                if remaining == "":
                    break
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    if not set(t).issubset(set(s)):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    result = []
    remaining = t
    
    while remaining:
        found = False
        
        # Try all possible substrings of s
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                substr = s[start:end+1]
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Check if direct match or reverse match with remaining
                if substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
                    
                if rev_substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed (reversed)
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Try to find a better match - maybe a shorter substring
            # Actually, since all chars exist, there should be a solution
            # Let's try a different approach: use the longest prefix that matches
            
            # Find the longest suffix of remaining that appears in s
            # as a substring or its reverse
            max_len = 0
            best_start, best_end = 0, 0
            
            for start in range(len(s)):
                for end in range(start, len(s)):
                    substr = s[start:end+1]
                    rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                    
                    # Check how much of remaining matches
                    match_len = 0
                    for i in range(min(len(remaining), len(substr))):
                        if substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            match_len += 1
                        elif rev_substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            match_len += 1
                        else:
                            break
                    
                    if match_len > max_len:
                        max_len = match_len
                        best_start, best_end = start, end
            
            if max_len > 0:
                substr = s[best_start:best_end+1]
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Check if this matches
                if substr == remaining[:max_len]:
                    result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
                    remaining = remaining[max_len:]
                elif rev_substr == remaining[:max_len]:
                    result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
                    remaining = remaining[max_len:]
            else:
                # This shouldn't happen if all chars are in s
                print(-1)
                return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)
```

Still not right. Let me implement a cleaner greedy solution:

```python
s = input().strip()
t = input().strip()

# Check if all characters in t exist in s
if not set(t).issubset(set(s)):
    print(-1)
else:
    result = []
    remaining = t
    
    while remaining:
        found = False
        
        # Try all possible substrings of s (both direct and reversed)
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                substr = s[start:end+1]
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Check if this substring (direct or reversed) matches the beginning of remaining
                if substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
                    
                if rev_substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Fallback: try to match a substring that can match a prefix
            # Find a substring that matches a prefix of remaining
            best_match = -1
            for start in range(len(s)):
                for end in range(start, len(s)):
                    substr = s[start:end+1]
                    rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                    
                    # Check if direct or reversed match with prefix
                    prefix_len = 0
                    for i in range(min(len(remaining), len(substr))):
                        if substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            prefix_len += 1
                        elif rev_substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            prefix_len += 1
                        else:
                            break
                    
                    if prefix_len > best_match:
                        best_match = prefix_len
                        best_start, best_end = start, end
            
            if best_match > 0:
                substr = s[best_start:best_end+1]
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                if best_match == len(remaining):
                    result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
                    remaining = ""
                elif best_match > 0:
                    result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
                    remaining = remaining[best_match:]
            else:
                print(-1)
                exit()
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)
```

I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me go back to the original problem and think simpler:

The key insight is that we need to build t from left to right. At each step, we're trying to match a prefix of t with some substring of s (either directly or reversed).

```python
s = input().strip()
t = input().strip()

# Check if all characters in t exist in s
if not set(t).issubset(set(s)):
    print(-1)
else:
    result = []
    remaining = t
    
    while remaining:
        # Try all possible substrings of s that can match the prefix of remaining
        found = False
        best_start, best_end = -1, -1
        
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                substr = s[start:end+1]
                rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Check if this substring matches remaining (direct or reversed)
                if substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
                    
                if rev_substr == remaining:
                    result.append((start + 1, end + 1))
                    remaining = ""
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Try to find a prefix match with some substring
            # Find the longest prefix that can be matched by some substring
            max_prefix_len = 0
            best_start, best_end = -1, -1
            
            for start in range(len(s)):
                for end in range(start, len(s)):
                    substr = s[start:end+1]
                    rev_substr = substr[::-1]
                    
                    # Check how much of remaining matches
                    prefix_len = 0
                    for i in range(min(len(remaining), len(substr))):
                        if substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            prefix_len += 1
                        elif rev_substr[i] == remaining[i]:
                            prefix_len += 1
                        else:
                            break
                    
                    if prefix_len > max_prefix_len:
                        max_prefix_len = prefix_len
                        best_start, best_end = start, end
            
            if max_prefix_len == 0:
                print(-1)
                exit()
            
            # Use the best match
            substr = s[best_start:best_end+1]
            rev_substr = substr[::-1]
            
            if substr == remaining[:max_prefix_len]:
                result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
                remaining = remaining[max_prefix_len:]
            elif rev_substr == remaining[:max_prefix_len]:
                result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
                remaining = remaining[max_prefix_len:]
            else:
                # This shouldn't happen
                print(-1)
                exit()
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of coating string `s`
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reversed order
3. We want to build target string `t` using the minimum number of such substrings
4. We need to output the positions (x,y) where x ≤ y means original order, x > y means reversed order

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains any character not present in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to find the minimum number of substrings from `s` that can form `t`
3. This is essentially a string reconstruction problem where we're replacing parts of `t` with substrings from `s`

## Algorithm Approach

1. First check if all characters in `t` exist in `s` - if not, return -1
2. Use dynamic programming or greedy approach to find the minimum number of pieces
3. For each position in `t`, try to match it with a substring from `s` that can be used in original or reversed form
4. We can use a greedy approach: try to extend as much as possible with each piece

## Step-by-step Solution

The key insight is that we can use a greedy matching strategy. For each position in the target string `t`, we try to match it with a substring from `s` that appears either in its original form or reversed form.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Find all possible substrings from s and their reverse
    # For each position in t, we'll try to find the best matching substring from s
    
    n = len(t)
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < n:
        # Try to find the longest substring from s that matches t[i:]
        # We can match either in original or reversed form
        
        found = False
        match_start = -1
        match_end = -1
        
        # Try all possible substrings from s
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start + 1, len(s) + 1):
                substr = s[start:end]
                reversed_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Check if this substring matches a prefix of t starting at position i
                # First check original form
                if len(substr) > 0 and t[i:i+len(substr)] == substr:
                    match_start = start
                    match_end = end - 1
                    found = True
                    break
                
                # Check reversed form
                if len(reversed_substr) > 0 and t[i:i+len(reversed_substr)] == reversed_substr:
                    match_start = start
                    match_end = end - 1
                    found = True
                    break
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Add the result
        if match_start <= match_end:
            result.append((match_start + 1, match_end + 1))  # Convert to 1-indexed
        else:
            result.append((match_end + 1, match_start + 1))  # Convert to 1-indexed
        
        i += len(s[match_start:match_end+1])
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    for char in t:
        if char not in s_set:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    # Greedy approach: at each step, find the longest possible substring from s that matches the current prefix of t
    # in either original or reversed form
    
    result = []
    i = 0
    n = len(t)
    
    while i < n:
        found = False
        
        # Try all possible substrings from s
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start + 1, len(s) + 1):
                substr = s[start:end]
                reversed_substr = substr[::-1]
                
                # Try matching in original form
                if len(substr) <= n - i and t[i:i + len(substr)] == substr:
                    result.append((start + 1, end))
                    i += len(substr)
                    found = True
                    break
                
                # Try matching in reversed form
                if len(reversed_substr) <= n - i and t[i:i + len(reversed_substr)] == reversed_substr:
                    result.append((end, start + 1))  # reversed: end > start
                    i += len(reversed_substr)
                    found = True
                    break
            
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A boy named Ayrat lives on planet AMI-1511. Each inhabitant of this planet has a talent. Specifically, Ayrat loves running, moreover, just running is not enough for him. He is dreaming of making running a real art.

First, he wants to construct the running track with coating t. On planet AMI-1511 the coating of the track is the sequence of colored blocks, where each block is denoted as the small English letter. Therefore, every coating can be treated as a string.

Unfortunately, blocks aren't freely sold to non-business customers, but Ayrat found an infinite number of coatings s. Also, he has scissors and glue. Ayrat is going to buy some coatings s, then cut out from each of them exactly one continuous piece (substring) and glue it to the end of his track coating. Moreover, he may choose to flip this block before glueing it. Ayrat want's to know the minimum number of coating s he needs to buy in order to get the coating t for his running track. Of course, he also want's to know some way to achieve the answer.


-----Input-----

First line of the input contains the string s — the coating that is present in the shop. Second line contains the string t — the coating Ayrat wants to obtain. Both strings are non-empty, consist of only small English letters and their length doesn't exceed 2100.


-----Output-----

The first line should contain the minimum needed number of coatings n or -1 if it's impossible to create the desired coating.

If the answer is not -1, then the following n lines should contain two integers x_{i} and y_{i} — numbers of ending blocks in the corresponding piece. If x_{i} ≤ y_{i} then this piece is used in the regular order, and if x_{i} > y_{i} piece is used in the reversed order. Print the pieces in the order they should be glued to get the string t.

Output

Input

ayrat

Output

1 1

8 7

Input
ami
no

Output
-1

In the second sample: "ayrat" = "a" + "yr" + "at".

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an infinite supply of a string `s` (the coating available in shop)
2. We can cut out any substring from `s` and either use it in original order or reversed order
3. We want to build target string `t` using the minimum number of such substrings
4. We need to output the positions (x,y) where x≤y means original order, x>y means reversed order

## Key Insights

1. If `t` contains characters not present in `s`, it's impossible → return -1
2. We need to find substrings from `s` that can form `t`
3. Since we can use any substring from `s`, we should use the shortest possible substrings
4. The greedy approach: try to match characters from `t` with the shortest possible substrings from `s`

## Algorithm

1. First check if all characters in `t` exist in `s`. If not, return -1
2. Use a greedy approach: for each position in `t`, find the shortest substring from `s` that can match the current segment
3. For each segment, try both normal and reversed versions to see which one matches
4. Use dynamic programming or greedy matching to minimize the number of pieces

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Validate feasibility**: Check if every character in `t` appears in `s`
2. **Find matches**: For each segment of `t`, find if it can be formed by a substring of `s` or its reverse
3. **Greedy selection**: Try to match as long as possible to minimize the number of pieces

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Find all possible substrings of s
    # For each position in t, try to match with shortest possible substring
    # We'll use greedy approach: match as much as possible
    
    result = []
    i = 0  # current position in t
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to match from current position i
        # Try to find a substring in s that can form t[i:]
        
        # For each possible starting point in s
        found = False
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                substr = s[start:end+1]
                
                # Check if substr or reversed substr can match t[i:]
                # First check original order
                if len(substr) == 0:
                    continue
                    
                # Check if substr can match the remaining part of t
                remain = t[i:]
                if len(substr) <= len(remain):
                    if substr == remain[:len(substr)]:
                        # Found a match in original order
                        result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed
                        i += len(substr)
                        found = True
                        break
                
                # Check if reversed substr can match the remaining part of t
                if len(substr) <= len(remain):
                    reversed_substr = substr[::-1]
                    if reversed_substr == remain[:len(substr)]:
                        # Found a match in reversed order
                        result.append((start + 1, end + 1))  # 1-indexed, since start > end
                        i += len(substr)
                        found = True
                        break
            if found:
                break
        
        if not found:
            # If no match found, check if full string t can be formed
            # This should not happen given our validation
            print(-1)
            return
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

Actually, let me think about this more systematically:

The key insight is that we want to minimize the number of pieces. We should try to match as long as possible with each piece.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # Check if all characters in t exist in s
    s_set = set(s)
    t_set = set(t)
    if not t_set.issubset(s_set):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # For each position in t, find the shortest substring of s that can match
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(t):
        # Try to find the longest possible match starting at position i
        max_match = 0
        best_start, best_end = -1, -1
        
        # For each possible substring in s
        for start in range(len(s)):
            for end in range(start, len(s)):
                substr = s[start:end+1]
                if len(substr) > len(t) - i:
                    continue
                    
                # Check if original match
                if substr == t[i:i+len(substr)]:
                    if len(substr) > max_match:
                        max_match = len(substr)
                        best_start, best_end = start, end
                
                # Check if reversed match
                reversed_substr = substr[::-1]
                if reversed_substr == t[i:i+len(substr)]:
                    if len(substr) > max_match:
                        max_match = len(substr)
                        best_start, best_end = start, end
        
        if max_match == 0:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # Add this piece
        result.append((best_start + 1, best_end + 1))
        i += max_match
    
    print(len(result))
    for x, y in result:
        print(x, y)

solve()
```

```python
s = input().strip()

            i += max_len
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.
3. The goal is to output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable, "0" otherwise.

## Key Insights

1. If two elements share a common prime factor, they can be swapped. This means elements that are "connected" through prime factors can be rearranged among themselves.
2. Elements that don't share any prime factors with each other cannot be swapped directly, so they form separate groups.
3. For a sequence to be coprime-sortable, the relative ordering of elements in each "connected component" must be compatible with the final sorted order.

## Approach

1. **Group elements by prime factors**: Elements that share at least one prime factor belong to the same group.
2. **Find connected components**: Use union-find or graph coloring to group elements that can reach each other through prime factor chains.
3. **Check if we can sort within each group**: For each connected component, check if the elements in their original positions can be rearranged to their correct sorted positions within that component.

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Find all prime factors for each number in the array
2. For each prime factor, group all numbers that have that prime factor
3. The connected components are the sets of numbers that share at least one prime factor (directly or indirectly through chains)
4. For each connected component, extract the elements and their original positions
5. Check if these elements can be rearranged to their correct sorted positions within the component

## Correct Approach

The key insight is that if two elements have a common prime factor, they can be swapped. This creates a graph where each element is a node, and there's an edge between two elements if they share a prime factor. 

Elements in the same connected component can be rearranged arbitrarily among themselves. 

For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, when we sort the array, the elements from each connected component must appear in the sorted array in a way that respects their original connectivity.

But more precisely: we can achieve any permutation within each connected component. So we can achieve the sorted order if and only if the multiset of elements in each connected component remains unchanged.

Wait, that's always true. The question is whether we can achieve the exact sorted order.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find all prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Find connected components
    # Use union-find or just group elements
    prime_to_elements = {}
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        for p in factors:
            if p not in prime_to_elements:
                prime_to_elements[p] = []
            prime_to_elements[p].append(i)
    
    # Build connected components: elements that share any prime factor
    from collections import defaultdict
    components = defaultdict(list)
    
    # For each prime, all elements having this prime form a component
    # Many elements can share multiple primes, so we need to union them
    # We'll build a graph and find connected components
    
    # First, find all elements that share a prime factor
    prime_to_indices = {}
    for p in prime_to_elements:
        prime_to_indices[p] = prime_to_elements[p]
    
    # Build graph: connect all elements that share a prime factor
    from collections import deque
    
    # Create adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    indices = set(range(n))
    
    # For each prime, connect all indices that have this prime
    for p, indices_with_p in prime_to_indices.items():
        for i in indices_with_p:
            for j in indices_with_p:
                if i != j:
                    adj[i].append(j)
    
    # Find connected components using BFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                component.append(node)
                
                for neighbor in adj[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            components.append(component)
    
    # For each component, check if we can achieve sorted order
    # Since we can rearrange freely within each component, 
    # we just need to check that the sorted array has the correct elements
    # Actually, we can always achieve the sorted order if we can permute within components
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that share a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they have at least one prime factor in common.
3. We need to output 1 if sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insights

1. If two numbers share a common prime factor, they can be swapped, which means they belong to the same "connected component" in a graph where nodes are numbers and edges exist between numbers sharing prime factors.

2. Within each connected component, we can rearrange elements in any order (since we can perform a series of swaps).

3. For the sequence to be sortable, each connected component must contain all elements that have the same prime factor pattern.

4. The critical insight: elements with the same prime factor can be rearranged freely within their component, but elements from different components cannot be directly swapped.

5. Therefore, for the sequence to be sortable, all elements that should end up in the same position in the final sorted array must be in the same connected component.

## Algorithm

1. Find all prime factors for each number in the array.
2. Group numbers by their prime factors (connected components).
3. For each connected component, check if the elements in that component appear in the correct relative positions in the sorted array.

Wait, let me reconsider. The key insight is actually simpler:

Since we can swap elements that share a common prime factor, elements that share any prime factor can be rearranged among themselves. So if two elements share a prime factor, they are in the same "swappable group".

The sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for every prime factor, all numbers that have that prime factor can be rearranged freely.

But actually, we need to think about connectivity: if we can form a connected component of numbers that share at least one prime factor, then all numbers in that component can be rearranged arbitrarily among themselves.

```python
def prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_list = []
    for num in a:
        prime_list.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build graph of connections
    # Each number is a node, connect if they share a common prime factor
    from collections import defaultdict, deque
    
    # Group indices by their prime factors
    indices_by_prime = defaultdict(list)
    for i, primes in enumerate(prime_list):
        for p in primes:
            indices_by_prime[p].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using union-find or DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for prime in indices_by_prime:
        indices = indices_by_prime[prime]
        if not indices:
            continue
        # Group indices that are connected through this prime
        # But we need to find all indices that are in the same component
        # Use union-find or DFS to find components
        pass
    
    # Actually, we can find connected components by considering that two indices are connected
    # if they share at least one prime factor
    from collections import defaultdict
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            # If they share a common prime factor
            if not prime_list[i] & prime_list[j]:
                continue
            adj[i].append(j)
            adj[j].append(i)
    
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            stack = [i]
            visited[i] = True
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                component.append(node)
                for neighbor in adj[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        stack.append(neighbor)
            components.append(component)
    
    # For each component, get the multiset of values in original array and in sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    result = True
    for comp in components:
        # Get values in original array at positions in comp
        original_values = [a[i] for i in comp]
        # Get values in sorted array at positions in comp
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in comp]
        
        # Check if multisets are the same
        from collections import Counter
        if Counter(original_values) != Counter(sorted_values):
            result = False
            break
    
    print(1 if result else 0)

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. We can only swap two numbers if they share at least one prime factor.
3. The goal is to check if we can achieve the sorted order through any sequence of such swaps.

## Key Insight

This problem is about connected components. Two numbers can be swapped if they share a prime factor. When two numbers with a common prime factor can be swapped, they belong to the same "connected component" in the graph where:
- Each number is a node
- Two numbers are connected if they share a prime factor

If all numbers in a connected component can be rearranged among themselves, then within that component, we can achieve any permutation of those elements.

For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, each connected component must contain all the elements that should end up in that component after sorting.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all prime factors of each number in the sequence
2. Group numbers by their shared prime factors (connected components)
3. For each connected component, collect all the numbers in it
4. Sort each component's numbers
5. Check if the sorted sequence matches the expected sorted sequence

## Detailed Approach

1. For each number, find all its prime factors
2. Build a graph where numbers are connected if they share any prime factor
3. Find connected components using DFS or union-find
4. For each component, check if it contains exactly the elements that should be in that position after sorting

## Implementation

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create sorted version
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # If the array is already sorted, return 1
    if arr == sorted_arr:
        return 1
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build connections - use union-find to group elements by shared prime factors
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each prime factor, union all numbers that have this prime factor
    # First, collect all unique prime factors
    all_primes = set()
    for factors in prime_factors:
        all_primes.update(factors)
    
    # For each prime, union all numbers that have this prime factor
    for prime in all_primes:
        prime_indices = []
        for i in range(n):
            if prime in prime_factors[i]:
                prime_indices.append(i)
        
        # If there are multiple numbers with this prime factor, union them all
        if len(prime_indices) > 1:
            for i in range(1, len(prime_indices)):
                union(prime_indices[0], prime_indices[i])
    
    # Group elements by their root parent (connected components)
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, check if it has exactly the elements that should be in that component
    # After sorting, the components should maintain their element count
    component_elements = {}
    for root in components:
        component_elements[root] = sorted([arr[i] for i in components[root]])
    
    # Now check if the sorted array can be formed by these components
    # For each component, the values in the position should match the sorted values in that component
    sorted_values = sorted(arr)
    
    # Check if we can assign the sorted values to components
    comp_to_values = {}
    for root in components:
        comp_to_values[root] = sorted([arr[i] for i in components[root]])
    
    # For each component, check if the values match what they should have in sorted order
    expected_values = []
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        # Find the sorted order of values in this component
        comp_val = comp_to_values[root]
        
        # Check if this position has the correct value
        # We need to assign sorted values to components
        pass

    # Therefore, the algorithm is:
    # 1. Find the sorted array
    # 2. Find connected components of positions where values at those positions share a common prime factor
    # 3. For each position i, check if the value a[i] and sorted_arr[i] are in the same component

    # Implementation:
    
    # Find sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # Build prime factors for each value in the original array
    prime_factors_arr = []
    for num in arr:
        factors = get_prime_factors(num)
        prime_factors_arr.append(factors)
    
    # Initialize parent array for union-find
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each value, check if it shares a prime factor with another value
    # For each prime factor, union all positions that have this prime factor
    for i in range(n):
        for prime in prime_factors_arr[i]:
            # Find all positions that have this prime factor
            for j in range(n):
                if i != j and prime in prime_factors_arr[j]:
                    union(i, j)
    
    # Check for each position i
    for i in range(n):
        # Get the actual value at position i and the target value at position i in sorted array

        target_val = sorted_arr[i]

        # The value 4 at position 1 needs to go to position 2 in sorted array (value 4).
```
