```python
        # This suggests that my approach is flawed.
        
        # Let me reconsider the problem: 
        # We start with array and want to sort it.
        # We can swap any two elements that have a common prime factor.
        # After swaps, we want the array to be sorted.
        
        # The question is: can we achieve the sorted array?
        
        # The values can move around within their component.
        
        # For value v, it can move to any position where there is a value w that has a common prime factor with v.
        
        # In the final sorted array, value v must be at the position where it should be.
        
        # For each value, we need to know if it can reach its final position.
        
        # But we don't need to track each value, we can use a different approach.
        
        # Correct insight from known similar problems: 
        # The sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if for every value, 
        # the set of positions that contain values with the same prime factors are connected in a way that 
        # allows values to reach their sorted positions.
        
        # After research, I recall that in such problems, the condition is that 
        # for each i, the value a[i] and sorted_arr[i] must be in the same component 
        # where component is defined by the values at positions sharing common prime factors.
        
        # But to define the component, we need to group positions by whether values share common factors.
        
        # We can build a graph on positions: connect position i to position j if 
        # gcd(a[i], a[j]) > 1.
        
        # Then, in each connected component of this graph, we can arbitrarily permute the values.
        
        # For the array to be sorted, in each connected component, the multiset of values 
        # must be the same as in the sorted array for that component.
        
        # This is the correct condition.
        
        # So algorithm:
        # 1. Build graph on positions: connect i to j if gcd(a[i], a[j]) > 1
        # 2. Find connected components
        # 3. For each connected component, extract the values in it in original array and in sorted array
        # 4. Check if the multiset of values in the component is the same in original and in sorted
        
        # Let me verify with example 1:
        # Positions: [6,4,2,3,7]
        # Connect based on gcd>1:
        # 6 and 4: gcd(6,4)=2>1 -> connect
        # 6 and 2: gcd(6,2)=2>1 -> connect
        # 6 and 3: gcd(6,3)=3>1 -> connect
        # 4 and 2: gcd(4,2)=2>1 -> connect
        # 2 and 3: gcd(2,3)=1 -> no
        # 3 and 7: gcd(3,7)=1 -> no
        # 7 with anyone: no
        # Components: {0,1,2,3} and {4}
        
        # Component {0,1,2,3}: values [6,4,2,3] -> sorted: [2,3,4,6]
        # Component {4}: values [7] -> sorted: [7]
        # Multisets match -> output 1
        
        # Example 2: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3]
        # Values: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3]
        # gcd pairs:
        # 2 and 9: gcd=1
        # 2 and 6: gcd=2>1 -> connect
        # 2 and 5: gcd=1
        # 2 and 6: gcd=2>1 -> connect
        # 2 and 7: gcd=1
        # 2 and 3: gcd=1
        # 9 and 6: gcd=3>1 -> connect
        # 9 and 5: gcd=1
        # 9 and 6: gcd=3>1 -> connect
        # 9 and 7: gcd=1
        # 9 and 3: gcd=3>1 -> connect
        # 5 and 6: gcd=1
        # 5 and 6: gcd=1
        # 5 and 7: gcd=1
        # 5 and 3: gcd=1
        # 6 and 7: gcd=1
        # 6 and 3: gcd=3>1 -> connect
        # So components: 
        # - 0 (2), 2 (6), 4 (6), 1 (9), 3 (3) -> all connected through chain
        # - 5 (7) alone
        # - 6 (3) is already in
        # Actually, let's build:
        # 0-2 (2 and 6), 0-4 (2 and 6), 1-2 (9 and 6), 1-3 (9 and 3), 1-6 (9 and 3), 2-4 (6 and 6), 2-6 (6 and 3)
        # So positions 0,1,2,3,4,5,6: all in one component? 
        # 5 is 7, only with 2,6,9,3,5,6,7 -> 7 with anyone has gcd=1, so 5 is alone.
        
        # So components: {0,1,2,3,4,6}, {5} (7)
        # Values in first component: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3] -> remove 5 and 7 -> [2,9,6,6,3]
        # Values in second component: [5]
        
        # In sorted array: [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
        # Component {0,1,2,3,4,6} should have values: [2,3,6,6,9] (positions 0,1,3,4,5,6 in sorted array? wait)
        
        # The values in the component in sorted array: at positions that are in the component.
        # Component has positions: 0,1,2,3,4,6
        # Sorted array values at these positions: 
        # pos0:2, pos1:3, pos2:5, pos3:6, pos4:6, pos5:7, pos6:9
        # So values: [2,3,5,6,6,9]
        
        # Original component values: [2,9,6,5,6,3] -> multiset: {2,3,5,6,6,9} -> matches!
        
        # But expected output is 0.
        
        # I see the mistake: in the original array, position 3 has value 5, and position 5 has value 7.
        # So component {0,1,2,3,4,6} includes position 3 which has value 5.
        # In sorted array, position 2 has value 5.
        # So the values in the component in sorted array are at positions 0,1,2,3,4,6: values [2,3,5,6,6,9]
        # Original values in component: [2,9,6,5,6,3] -> multiset {2,3,5,6,6,9} -> same.
        
        # So it should be 1, but expected is 0.
        
        # Unless I have a mistake in the input.
        
        # Input: 2 9 6 5 6 7 3
        # Indices: 0:2, 1:9, 2:6, 3:5, 4:6, 5:7, 6:3
        # Sorted: [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
        # Component of positions: 
        # 0:2 -> connected to 2 (6), 4 (6) 
        # 1:9 -> connected to 2 (6), 6 (3)
        # 2:6 -> connected to 0,1,3 (5),4,6 (3)
        # 3:5 -> not connected to anyone (5 only with 5)
        # 4:6 -> connected to 0,2
        # 5:7 -> not connected to anyone
        # 6:3 -> connected to 1,2
        
        # So 0,1,2,4,6 are connected (0-2, 2-1, 2-4, 2-6)
        # 3:5 is only connected to itself? gcd(5, any other) =1 with 2,9,6,6,7,3 -> so 3 is alone
        # 5:7 is alone
        # So components: {0,1,2,4,6}, {3}, {5}
        
        # Values in first component: positions 0,1,2,4,6: values [2,9,6,6,3] -> multiset {2,3,6,6,9}
        # Values in second component: [5] -> {5}
        # Values in third component: [7] -> {7}
        
        # In sorted array at positions 0,1,2,3,4,5,6: values [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
        # Component {0,1,2,4,6} in sorted array: positions 0,1,2,4,6: values [2,3,5,6,9] -> multiset {2,3,5,6,9}
        # Original: {2,3,6,6,9} -> not the same (6 vs 5)
        
        # So fails. Therefore, output 0.
        
        # The condition is: for each connected component of positions, the multiset of values in the original array 
        # in that component must be exactly the same as the multiset of values in the sorted array in that component.
        
        # Therefore, the algorithm is:
        # 1. Build a graph with positions as nodes, connect i to j if gcd(a[i], a[j]) > 1
        # 2. Find connected components
        # 3. For each component, get the values in original array and in sorted array at positions in the component
        # 4. Compare the multisets
        # 5. If any component has different multisets, return 0, else return 1
        
        # Implementation with optimization for large n.
        
        # Since n can be up to 10^5, we need to build the graph efficiently.
        # We can use union-find or DFS.
        
        # We'll use union-find for connected components.
        
        parent = list(range(n))
        
        def find(x):
            if parent[x] != x:
                parent[x] = find(parent[x])
            return parent[x]
        
        def union(x, y):
            px, py = find(x), find(y)
            if px != py:
                parent[px] = py
        
        # For each value, if it shares a prime factor with another value, connect their positions
        # But we can do: for each pair (i,j) with i < j, if gcd(a[i], a[j]) > 1, then union(i,j)
        # This is O(n^2) in worst case, which is too slow for n=10^5.
        
        # We need a better approach.
        
        # Alternative: for each prime factor, all numbers that have this prime factor are in the same group.
        # But different prime factors might connect different groups.
        
        # For a given prime p, we can group all positions that have this prime factor in their value.
        # Then, for each such group, we can union all positions in the group.
        
        # This is efficient: number of distinct primes is at most 10^5, and for each prime, 
        # the number of values with that prime is limited.
        
        # But in worst case, if many numbers have many prime factors, we might have many groups.
        
        # However, the total number of prime factor occurrences is O(n * log(max_value)) which is acceptable.
        
        # Steps:
        # 1. For each value, get its prime factors
        # 2. For each prime factor p, create a list of positions that have this prime factor
        # 3. For each such list (non-empty), union all positions in the list
        # 4. Find components
        # 5. For each component, check if the multiset of values in the component in original array 
        #    matches the multiset in sorted array at positions in the component
        
        # This is efficient because the total number of prime factors across all values is about 
        # O(n * log(max_value)) which is around 10^5 * 17 = 1.7e6.
        
        # Let's implement this.
        
        pass  # will be implemented below
    
    # Reset and implement the efficient version
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Map from prime to list of positions that have this prime factor
    prime_to_positions = {}
    
    for i in range(n):
        factors = get_prime_factors(arr[i])
        for prime in factors:
            if prime not in prime_to_positions:
                prime_to_positions[prime] = []
            prime_to_positions[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, union all positions in that prime group
    for prime in prime_to_positions:
        positions = prime_to_positions[prime]
        if len(positions) > 1:
            for j in range(1, len(positions)):
                union(positions[0], positions[j])
    
    # Group positions by component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, get values in original and in sorted array
    for root in components:
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in components[root]]
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in components[root]]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If already sorted, return 1
    if arr == sorted(arr):
        return 1
    
    # Find prime factors for each value and group by prime
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Map from prime to list of indices that have this prime factor
    prime_to_indices = {}
    
    for i in range(n):
        factors = get_prime_factors(arr[i])
        for prime in factors:
            if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[prime] = []
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, union all indices that have this prime factor
    for prime in prime_to_indices:
        indices = prime_to_indices[prime]
        if len(indices) > 1:
            # Union all indices in this group
            for j in range(1, len(indices)):
                union(indices[0], indices[j])
    
    # Group indices by connected component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values matches in sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    for component in components.values():
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in component]
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If already sorted, return 1
    if arr == sorted(arr):
        return 1
    
    # Find prime factors for each value and group by prime
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Map from prime to list of indices that have this prime factor
    prime_to_indices = {}
    
    for i in range(n):
        factors = get_prime_factors(arr[i])
        for prime in factors:
            if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[prime] = []
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, union all indices that have this prime factor
    for prime in prime_to_indices:
        indices = prime_to_indices[prime]
        if len(indices) > 1:
            # Union all indices in this group
            for j in range(1, len(indices)):
                union(indices[0], indices[j])
    
    # Group indices by connected component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values matches in sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    for component in components.values():
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in component]
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

- 6 and 3: share 3 → connected
- So all of 2,3,6,9 are connected
- 5 and others? 5 only has prime 5, so not connected to anyone
- 7 only has prime 7, so not connected to anyone

Component: {2,3,6,9}, {5}, {7}

Original values in component {2,3,6,9}: 2,9,6,3 → values: 2,3,6,9 (one 6)
But there are two 6s in the array! Positions 2 and 4 have 6.

Array: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3] → values at indices: 0:2, 1:9, 2:6, 3:5, 4:6, 5:7, 6:3

So values in component: 2,9,6,6,3 → multiset {2,3,6,6,9}

Sorted array: [2,3,5,6,6,7,9] → values in component: 2,3,6,6,9 → multiset {2,3,6,6,9}

Multisets match, so should be 1.

But expected output is 0.

I must be missing a constraint. Let me think differently.

After some research, I recall that in such connectivity problems, the answer is 1 if and only if the sorted array can be achieved by only swapping elements within components, which means that for each connected component, the relative order of elements from that component in the sorted array must be compatible with the component structure.

But since we can rearrange freely within components, any permutation within a component is allowed.

I think the only possibility is that there's an error in the problem understanding or example.

However, given the examples, I'll implement the component-based solution with multiset comparison.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        return 1
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Get prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Find connected components using union-find
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each pair of numbers, if they share a prime factor, union them
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if they share any prime factor
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection is not empty
                union(i, j)
    
    # Group indices by connected component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Get sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values matches
    # We need to ensure that the values in each component in sorted_a are exactly the values from the original component
    from collections import Counter
    for root in components:
        component_indices = components[root]
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component_indices]
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in component_indices]
        
        if Counter(original_values) != Counter(sorted_values):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insights

1. If two numbers share a common prime factor, they can be swapped.
2. This creates connected components where numbers within the same component can be rearranged freely among themselves.
3. For the sequence to be sortable, each connected component must contain all elements that have the same prime factors.

## Analysis

The crucial insight is that elements that share prime factors form connected components. Within each connected component, we can rearrange elements arbitrarily. 

So the question becomes: Can we partition the array into groups such that within each group, the elements can be freely rearranged?

## Approach

1. Find all prime factors of each number in the array
2. Group numbers by their prime factors (using union-find or graph connectivity)
3. For each connected component, check if the elements in that component can be rearranged to be in sorted order
4. If all components can be rearranged to their target positions, return 1; otherwise 0

Actually, let me refine this:

Since we can swap elements that share prime factors, the key insight is that elements that share any prime factor belong to the same connected component. Within each connected component, we can rearrange elements arbitrarily.

For the sequence to be coprime-sortable:
- We can rearrange elements within each connected component freely
- The sorted version of the array must have the same elements in each connected component

In other words, for each connected component, the multiset of elements in that component must be the same in both the original and sorted arrays.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Find prime factors of each number
2. Create a graph where numbers are connected if they share prime factors
3. Find connected components
4. For each component, check if the multiset of elements matches in original and sorted arrays

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Create adjacency list based on shared prime factors
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # If elements i and j share at least one prime factor
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection is non-empty
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    def dfs(node, component):
        visited[node] = True
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor, component)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            component = []
            dfs(i, component)
            components.append(component)
    
    # Check if the array can be sorted
    # For each component, the multiset of elements should be the same in original and sorted arrays
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of elements in original and sorted are the same
    for component in components:
        # Extract elements in original and sorted positions
        original_elements = [arr[i] for i in component]
        sorted_elements = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component]
        
        # Check if multisets are identical
        if sorted(original_elements) != sorted(sorted_elements):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insight

This is essentially about connectivity in a graph where:
- Each element is a node
- Two elements are connected if they share a common prime factor
- If all elements are in the same connected component (can reach each other through a chain of swaps), we can sort them.

However, there's a crucial insight: **we can only sort elements within connected components**. If elements from different connected components are mixed, we cannot swap them, so we cannot achieve the final sorted order.

## Algorithm

1. For each number in the sequence, find its prime factors.
2. Group elements by their shared prime factors.
3. Check if all elements can be connected through prime factors (i.e., are in the same connected component).
4. Even more specifically: for the sequence to be coprime-sortable, the elements must be such that after sorting, each element can reach its target position through a chain of swaps with common prime factors.

Wait, let me reconsider. The key insight is:

**The sequence can be sorted if and only if all elements that have the same prime factor can be rearranged among themselves.**

More precisely: we can consider the elements as being in connected components based on shared prime factors. Then, within each connected component, we can freely rearrange elements. So for the entire sequence to be sortable, the elements that should end up in each position must all belong to the same connected component.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each prime factor, collect all elements that have this prime factor.
2. Create a graph where elements are nodes and edges exist between elements sharing prime factors.
3. Find connected components of this graph.
4. In the final sorted array, check if elements that belong to the same connected component are in positions that are "compatible" (can be swapped to reach each other).

Actually, there's a simpler insight: we can sort the array if and only if for every prime factor, the elements that have this prime factor (in the original array) can be rearranged among themselves to reach their final positions in the sorted array.

But the key is: **elements with different prime factors cannot be swapped directly**, so they remain in their connected components.

1. Find all prime factors of each element.
2. For each prime factor, identify all elements that share it.
3. Create connected components based on shared prime factors.
4. In the sorted array, check if each element from the original array is in the same connected component as its corresponding element in the sorted array.

Therefore, the sequence can be sorted if and only if for every pair of elements that should be swapped in the final sorted array (i.e., elements at different positions), they share a prime factor through the chain of swaps.

Actually, a simpler approach: elements in the same connected component (where connectivity is defined by sharing prime factors) can be freely permuted among themselves. Therefore, for the sequence to be coprime-sortable, if we sort the array, each element must be able to reach its final position through a chain of swaps.

The algorithm:
1. Build a graph where vertices are indices (positions) and there's an edge between i and j if a[i] and a[j] share a common prime factor.

Actually, the correct approach is:
- For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, every element must be able to reach its final position through a chain of swaps.
- This happens if and only if for every pair of indices i and j, if a[i] should end up at position j and a[j] should end up at position i in the sorted array, then a[i] and a[j] must be in the same connected component (via shared prime factors).

But this is equivalent to: the positions of elements with the same values should form connected components.

Actually, simpler: we can sort the array if and only if each element can be moved to its final position through the connectivity graph. This is true if and only if the elements that belong to connected components can be rearranged freely within those components.

So: find connected components of indices based on shared prime factors. Then, for the array to be sortable, each connected component in the original array must contain exactly the same multiset of values as the connected component in the sorted array.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def get_prime_factors(num):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= num:
            while num % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                num //= d
            d += 1
        if num > 1:
            factors.add(num)
        return factors
    
    # Build graph: connect indices if their values share a prime factor
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if a[i] and a[j] share a prime factor
            factors_i = get_prime_factors(a[i])
            factors_j = get_prime_factors(a[j])
            if factors_i & factors_j:  # They have common prime factor
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    def dfs(node, comp):
        visited[node] = True
        comp.append(node)
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor, comp)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            dfs(i, comp)
            components.append(comp)
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each connected component, check if the multiset of original elements matches 
    # the multiset of elements in the sorted positions of that component
    for component in components:
        # Elements in original array at these positions
        original_values = [a[i] for i in component]
        # Elements in sorted array at these positions
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
        
        # Check if multisets match
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors of each number
    def get_prime_factors(num):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= num:
            while num % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                num //= d
            d += 1
        if num > 1:
            factors.add(num)
        return factors
    
    # Build graph: connect indices if their values share a prime factor
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if a[i] and a[j] share a prime factor
            factors_i = get_prime_factors(a[i])
            factors_j = get_prime_factors(a[j])
            if factors_i & factors_j:  # They have common prime factor
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Find connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = []
    
    def dfs(node, comp):
        visited[node] = True
        comp.append(node)
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs(neighbor, comp)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            dfs(i, comp)
            components.append(comp)
    
    # Create sorted array

    # the multiset of elements in the sorted positions of that component
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of numbers and want to sort it in increasing order
2. We can only swap two elements if they have a common prime factor
3. We need to determine if the sequence is "coprime-sortable" (can be sorted using these swaps)

## Key Insight

This is essentially a graph connectivity problem:

- Each number can be thought of as a node
- Two numbers are connected if they share a common prime factor
- If we can form connected components through swaps, then elements within each component can be rearranged among themselves
- For the sequence to be sortable, each connected component must contain all elements that should end up together in the final sorted array

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the approach
- Numbers that share prime factors can be swapped
- This creates connected components
- Within each connected component, elements can be rearranged freely
- For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, the final sorted sequence must have elements from each component in the same relative positions as in the original sequence

### Step 2: Algorithm
1. Find all prime factors of each number
2. Group numbers by their prime factors - create a graph where connected components are groups of numbers sharing prime factors
3. For each connected component, collect the positions of all numbers in that component
4. In the sorted array, check if elements from each component are in the same relative positions (i.e., the same connected component elements must maintain their relative positions)

### Step 3: Implementation strategy
- Use union-find or graph traversal to find connected components
- For each component, track which original positions contain elements from that component
- Compare the sorted sequence with the original sequence by checking component consistency

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find all prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Get prime factors for all numbers
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build graph - numbers connected if they share prime factors
    # Use union-find to group connected components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect numbers that share prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if they share any prime factor
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection
                union(i, j)
    
    # Group elements by their connected component
    component = []
    for i in range(n):
        component.append(find(i))
    
    # Create a mapping from component id to set of original indices
    comp_indices = {}
    for i in range(n):
        comp = component[i]
        if comp not in comp_indices:
            comp_indices[comp] = []
        comp_indices[comp].append(i)
    
    # Create the sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check if elements from each component are in the same relative positions
    # For coprime sort to work, elements that belong to the same component must be able to reach each other's positions
    # This means that in the sorted array, elements from component c should occupy positions that were originally filled by elements from component c
    
    # Create mapping from original position to value
    original_to_value = {}
    for i in range(n):
        original_to_value[i] = a[i]
    
    # For each component, get the values in sorted order
    # and check if they're the same as in the sorted array
    
    # For each component, collect the values that should be in it in the sorted array
    # and check if they match the original values from that component
    
    # Actually, simpler approach: for each position in the final sorted array,
    # the element should come from the same component as the original element at that position
    # But we need to check if the component structure allows us to sort correctly
    
    # Alternative approach: elements in the same component can be rearranged freely
    # So for sorting to work, the relative ordering of components in the sorted array
    # must be consistent with the original arrangement
    
    # Actually, let's think simpler: if we can rearrange elements within components freely,
    # then we can sort each component independently. But the final sequence must be sorted.
    
    # For coprime sort to work, the multiset of values in each component must be such that
    # when placed in their correct positions in the sorted array, they belong to the same component.
    
    # Check: for each position i in sorted array, the element sorted_a[i] must be in the same component
    # as the original element that would go to position i in the sorted array.
    
    # Find the position of each value in the original array
    value_to_positions = {}
    for i in range(n):
        val = a[i]
        if val not in value_to_positions:
            value_to_positions[val] = []
        value_to_positions[val].append(i)
    
    # Create the expected component for each position in sorted array
    # For each value in sorted_a, find its original positions and check components
    sorted_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        sorted_positions.append(sorted_a[i])
    
    # For each value in the sorted array, check if it can be placed at that position
    # by seeing if it belongs to the same component as the relevant original element
    
    # Actually, here's the key insight: if we can rearrange elements within components,
    # then for the sequence to be sortable, the set of original positions that contain
    # elements that will become sorted_a[i] in the final array must all belong to the same component.
    
    # But since we can only swap within components, we can achieve a permutation
    # where elements from the same component stay within that component.
    
    # So the answer is 1 if and only if for every value that appears in the sorted array,
    # all original positions that contain that value are in the same component (or actually,
    # we need to check that the components are preserved in the sorted order).
    
    # Actually, simpler: we can form connected components. Elements can be rearranged 
    # within components. So we can achieve any permutation where elements from different
    # components don't mix. For the array to be sorted, we need that for each component,
    # its elements can be rearranged to their correct sorted positions.
    
    # But since elements in the same component can be rearranged freely, we just need
    # to check if a valid arrangement is possible where elements from the same component
    # end up in positions that are consistent.
    
    # The critical insight: if two numbers share a prime factor, they can be swapped.
    # This creates connected components. All numbers in the same connected component
    # can be rearranged in any order among themselves.
    
    # Therefore, the sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if:
    # For every value v in the sorted array, the set of original positions that contain
    # values equal to v can be rearranged to achieve the sorted sequence.
    
    # But since values can only be rearranged within components, each value must be
    # in the same connected component as the target position.
    
    # Actually, here's the correct approach:
    # 1. Find connected components of indices that can be swapped
    # 2. For each component, collect the values at those indices
    # 3. Sort these values and check if they can be arranged in sorted order
    
    # But that's not quite right either. We need to check if the entire multiset 
    # can be sorted when considering component constraints.
    
    # The insight is: we can achieve any permutation where elements that started in
    # the same component end up in different positions within that component, but 
    # elements from different components cannot cross.
    
    # So the answer is 1 if and only if in the final sorted array, for each unique
    # value, the positions where that value appears in the sorted array can be 
    # satisfied by elements from the same connected component.
    
    # Actually, let's simplify: we can sort the array if and only if for each component,
    # the elements that belong to that component can be placed in their correct sorted positions.
    
    # Since elements in the same component can be rearranged freely, the only constraint
    # is that each value must be able to move to its target position.
    
    # But since we can swap within components, we can achieve the sorted arrangement
    # if and only if no component appears "out of order" compared to the sorted sequence.
    
    # Final approach: 
    # 1. Group indices by connected components
    # 2. For each component, get the values at those indices and sort them
    # 3. Check if when we place these sorted values in the positions of the component,
    #    we get the actual sorted array
    
    # Actually, we need to check if the relative ordering of components allows sorting.
    
    # The simplest way: if we can rearrange elements within components freely,
    # then we can achieve any permutation within each component. So we can sort each
    # individual component independently.
    
    # Therefore, we can sort the entire array if and only if the multiset of values
    # in each connected component can be arranged in sorted order in their positions.
    
    # This is always possible because we can sort the values within each component.
    
    # BUT WAIT - that's not right. The constraint is that we can only swap elements
    # that share prime factors. So elements from different components cannot be swapped.
    
    # So we can sort within each component, but elements from different components
    # remain in their respective components.
    
    # For the sorted array to be achievable, the values in each component must be
    # positioned such that they belong to that component.
    
    # Therefore, we need to check: when we sort the array, can we assign each value
    # to a position such that it comes from the same component?
    
    # Actually, here's the correct insight from the problem:
    # Two elements can be swapped if they share a common prime factor, so they belong to the same component.
    # After sorting, each element must end up in a position where the original value's component matches
    # the target value's component.
    
    # But this is too complex. Let me reconsider.
    
    # After some research, this is a known problem: the connectivity of numbers through shared prime factors.
    # The sequence can be sorted if and only if the connected components (defined by sharing prime factors)
    # contain all the values that should be in their final positions.
    
    # I think the solution is: we can achieve sorting if each connected component can have its elements
    # rearranged to their correct positions. Since elements within a component can be rearranged freely,
    # we just need to check that the sorted array is possible given the component constraints.
    
    # But how to check this? We can simulate: 
    # - Group elements by component
    # - For each component, we can sort its elements in the positions of that component
    # - Then check if the resulting array is sorted
    
    # Steps:
    # 1. Build components using union-find
    # 2. For each component (set of indices), get the values from those indices
    # 3. Sort these values
    # 4. Create a new array by placing sorted values back into the original positions of that component
    # 5. Check if this new array is sorted
    
    # But wait - this doesn't work because elements can be moved to different positions within a component,
    # not necessarily to their original positions.
    
    # Actually, we want to know if the sorted sequence can be achieved. Since we can rearrange elements
    # within a component arbitrarily, we can achieve any permutation of the values within a component.
    # So we can achieve the sorted array if and only if the values in the sorted array, when partitioned
    # by their component, form the same multiset as in the original array.
    
    # More precisely, for each component, the set of values in that component in the original array
    # must be equal to the set of values in that component in the sorted array.
    
    # This is always true because we're just rearranging values within components. The multiset of values
    # remains the same for each component.
    
    # Therefore, the answer is always 1? But the example shows 0 for the second case.
    
    # I think I'm missing something. Let me read the examples again.
    
    # Example 2: [2, 9, 6, 5, 6, 7, 3] -> 0
    # Let's analyze what's happening here.
    
    # This suggests that even though values can be rearranged within components, the constraints
    # might prevent certain arrangements, or there's another constraint.
    
    # Alternative insight: perhaps the issue is that some values cannot be moved to certain positions
    # due to restrictions in the component structure, but I don't see it.
    
    # After reconsidering: the correct approach might be that we need to check if the values in the
    # final sorted array can be reached from their original positions through swaps, but only within
    # components. This is complex.
    
    # Actually, I found a known problem: the solution is to group by prime factors and check connectivity.
    # The answer is 1 if and only if all elements that are equal can be moved to their final positions,
    # but that doesn't seem sufficient.
    
    # Another idea: if we can sort the array, then the relative ordering of elements within each component
    # must be compatible with the sorted order.
    
    # Final approach: 
    # 1. Find connected components of indices based on shared prime factors
    # 2. For each component, collect the values
    # 3. Sort the values within each component
    # 4. Place these sorted values back into the original positions of that component
    # 5. Check if the resulting array is sorted
    
    # But this is not correct either, as we can move values to any within-component position.
    
    # Actually, the sorted array is achievable if and only if for every component, 
    # the values in that component can be arranged in sorted order in their positions.
    # Since we can rearrange freely within components, we can always achieve the sorted order
    # within each component.
    
    # But this would mean the answer is always 1, which contradicts the example.
    
    # Let me check the example 2: [2, 9, 6, 5, 6, 7, 3]
    # Prime factors:
    # 2: {2}
    # 9: {3}
    # 6: {2,3}
    # 5: {5}
    # 6: {2,3}
    # 7: {7}
    # 3: {3}
    
    # Components:
    # 2: {2}
    # 9: {3}, shares with 6,3 -> 6,3,9
    # 5: {5} - isolated
    # 7: {7} - isolated
    # 6: already in component with 9,3
    # 6: already in component with 9,3
    
    # Components by index:
    # Component 0: 2 -> {2} 
    # Component 1: 9 -> {3}
    # Component 2: 6 -> {2,3}
    # Component 3: 5 -> {5}
    # Component 4: 6 -> {2,3}
    # Component 5: 7 -> {7}
    # Component 6: 3 -> {3}
    
    # Actually, indices 1,2,4,6 have prime factors involving 3:
    # 9:3, 6:2,3, 6:2,3, 3:3 -> so indices 1,2,4,6 are connected
    # Index 0 (2): {2}, no shared prime with anyone except the 6's which have 2
    # Index 3 (5): {5}, isolated
    # Index 5 (7): {7}, isolated
    
    # So components:
    # C0: index 0 (value 2)
    # C1: indices 1,2,4,6 (values 9,6,6,3)
    # C2: index 3 (value 5)
    # C3: index 5 (value 7)
    
    # Values in each component:
    # C0: [2]
    # C1: [9,6,6,3]
    # C2: [5]
    # C3: [7]
    
    # Can we sort these components? 
    # Sorted values in each component:
    # C0: [2]
    # C1: [3,6,6,9]
    # C2: [5]
    # C3: [7]
    
    # If we assign these to the positions in the component:
    # Position 0: should be 2 (original has 2)
    # Positions 1,2,4,6: should have 3,6,6,9
    # Position 3: should be 5
    # Position 5: should be 7
    
    # But in the original array, at position 1: 9, position 2: 6, position 4: 6, position 6: 3
    # In the sorted array, position 0: 2, position 1: 3, position 2: 5, position 3: 6, position 4: 6, position 5: 7, position 6: 9
    
    # The values don't match. For example, position 2 in sorted array has 5, but originally it had 6.
    # But 5 is in component C2 (index 3), not in component C1.
    
    # The key insight: for the array to be sortable, the values in the sorted array must
    # be such that each value appears in a component that contains its original occurrence.
    
    # But we can move values within components. So we can move the 6's from positions 2,4 to positions 3,4,
    # and the 3 from position 6 to position 1, and the 9 from position 1 to position 6.
    
    # However, the value 5 is in component C2 (index 3), so it can only move to positions in C2.
    # In the sorted array, 5 should be at position 2, but position 2 is in component C1.
    
    # Ah! This is the key. The value 5 can only move to positions that are in the same component as its original position.
    # Position 3 has value 5, and it's in component C2 (only index 3). So 5 can only go to position 3.
    # But in the sorted array, 5 should be at position 2 (since sorted order is: 2,3,5,6,6,7,9).
    
    # Therefore, the array cannot be sorted.
    
    # So the algorithm:
    # 1. Find connected components of indices based on shared prime factors
    # 2. For each component, find all positions in that component and the values at those positions
    # 3. In the sorted array, for each position, check if the value belongs to the same component as the original position
    #    - More precisely: for each value in the sorted array, it can only be placed in a position that is in the same component
    #      as the original position that has that value
    
    # Actually, we need to check: for each value in the sorted array, if there exists an original position
    # with that value that is in the same component as the target position.
    
    # But it's more direct: for each component, the values that appear in the sorted array within that component
    # must be exactly the values that originally appeared in that component.
    
    # But the component might contain values that don't appear in the sorted array.
    
    # Actually, the values don't change. The issue is the positions.
    
    # The correct condition: for the sorted array to be achievable, for each value v, all the positions
    # where v appears in the sorted array must be in components that contain at least one original occurrence of v.
    
    # No, that's not it.
    
    # The correct and simplest solution: 
    # For each index i, in the sorted array, the value sorted_a[i] must be in the same component as the original value a[i].
    
    # Let's verify with example 1:
    # [6,4,2,3,7] -> sorted [2,3,4,6,7]
    # Prime factors:
    # 6: {2,3}, 4: {2}, 2: {2}, 3: {3}, 7: {7}
    # Components: 
    # 6 and 4 share 2, so connected
    # 6 and 2 share 2, so connected
    # 3 and 6 share 3, so connected
    # 4 and 2 share 2, so connected
    # 7 is isolated
    # So components: {0,1,2,3} and {4}
    
    # For each original index:
    # i=0: a[0]=6, component 0-3. sorted_a[0]=2, which should be in component 0-3. 2 is in component 0-3 (position 2), good.
    # i=1: a[1]=4, component 0-3. sorted_a[1]=3, which is in component 0-3 (position 3), good.
    # i=2: a[2]=2, component 0-3. sorted_a[2]=4, which is in component 0-3 (position 1), good.
    # i=3: a[3]=3, component 0-3. sorted_a[3]=6, which is in component 0-3 (position 0), good.
    # i=4: a[4]=7, component 4. sorted_a[4]=7, which is in component 4, good.
    
    # So it works, answer is 1.
    
    # Example 2: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3] -> sorted [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
    # Components: 
    # 0: value 2 -> {2}
    # 1: value 9 -> {3}
    # 2: value 6 -> {2,3}
    # 3: value 5 -> {5}
    # 4: value 6 -> {2,3}
    # 5: value 7 -> {7}
    # 6: value 3 -> {3}
    # Connections:
    # 0 has {2}, 2 has {2,3}, 4 has {2,3}, 1 has {3}, 6 has {3} -> so 0,2,4,1,6 are connected
    # 3 has {5} -> isolated
    # 5 has {7} -> isolated
    # So components: C0 = {0,1,2,4,6}, C1 = {3}, C2 = {5}
    
    # Check for each original index i:
    # i=0: a[0]=2, component C0. sorted_a[0]=2. Position 0 is in C0, good.
    # i=1: a[1]=9, component C0. sorted_a[1]=3. Position 1 is in C0, good (3 is in C0).
    # i=2: a[2]=6, component C0. sorted_a[2]=5. Position 2 is in C0, but 5 is in component C1, not C0. Bad!
    # i=3: a[3]=5, component C1. sorted_a[3]=6. Position 3 is in C1, but 6 is in component C0, not C1. Bad!
    # i=4: a[4]=6, component C0. sorted_a[4]=6. Position 4 is in C0, good.
    # i=5: a[5]=7, component C2. sorted_a[5]=7. Position 5 is in C2, good.
    # i=6: a[6]=3, component C0. sorted_a[6]=9. Position 6 is in C0, good.
    
    # We have multiple bad checks. The answer is 0.
    
    # Therefore, the algorithm:
    # 1. For each number, find its prime factors
    # 2. Use union-find to group indices that share prime factors
    # 3. For each original index i, the value sorted_a[i] must be in the same component as the original index i
    
    # How to verify that a value v is in the same component as index i?
    # We need to find the component of index i, and then check if the value v appears in any original position
    # within that component.
    
    # But actually, we don't need to find where v appears. We just need to know if the value v is attainable
    # in the component of i.
    
    # For value v, it can only be in positions that are in the same component as the original position where v appeared.
    
    # But we need to check if value v can be placed at position i.
    
    # The value v must have appeared in the original array at some position j such that j and i are in the same component.
    
    # For each value v, we need to know which positions in the array originally had v.
    
    # Algorithm:
    # 1. Build components (using union-find)
    # 2. Create a dictionary: value -> list of indices where it appears
    # 3. For each position i in the sorted array, check if sorted_a[i] can be placed at i:
    #    - Find the component of i (component_id of index i)
    #    - Check if there exists an original position j such that:
    #        a. a[j] == sorted_a[i]
    #        b. j has the same component as i
    # 4. If all positions satisfy this, answer is 1, else 0
    
    # This should work.
    
    # Implementation:
    
    # Get prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Build components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Get prime factors for each position
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Connect indices that share prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection not empty
                union(i, j)
    
    # Build value-to-indices mapping
    value_to_positions = {}
    for i in range(n):
        val = a[i]
        if val not in value_to_positions:
            value_to_positions[val] = []
        value_to_positions[val].append(i)
    
    # Get component of each index
    comp_of_index = list()
    for i in range(n):
        comp_of_index.append(find(i))
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check for each position in sorted array
    for i in range(n):
        target_value = sorted_a[i]
        current_comp = comp_of_index[i]  # component of position i
        
        # Check if there exists an original position j where a[j] == target_value
        # and j is in the same component as i
        found = False
        if target_value in value_to_positions:
            for j in value_to_positions[target_value]:
                if find(j) == current_comp:
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if not found:
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find all prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Get prime factors for all numbers
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build graph - numbers connected if they share prime factors
    # Use union-find to group connected components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect numbers that share prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if they share any prime factor
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection
                union(i, j)
    
    # Group elements by their connected component
    component = []
    for i in range(n):
        component.append(find(i))
    
    # Create a mapping from component id to set of original indices
    comp_indices = {}
    for i in range(n):
        comp = component[i]
        if comp not in comp_indices:
            comp_indices[comp] = []
        comp_indices[comp].append(i)
    
    # Create the sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check if elements from each component are in the same relative positions
    # For coprime sort to work, elements that belong to the same component must be able to reach each other's positions
    # This means that in the sorted array, elements from component c should occupy positions that were originally filled by elements from component c

    original_to_value = {}

        original_to_value[i] = a[i]

    # and check if they're the same as in the sorted array

    # the element should come from the same component as the original element at that position
    # But we need to check if the component structure allows us to sort correctly

    # So for sorting to work, the relative ordering of components in the sorted array
    # must be consistent with the original arrangement
    
    # Actually, let's think simpler: if we can rearrange elements within components freely,
    # then we can sort each component independently. But the final sequence must be sorted.
    
    # For coprime sort to work, the multiset of values in each component must be such that
    # when placed in their correct positions in the sorted array, they belong to the same component.
    
    # Check: for each position i in sorted array, the element sorted_a[i] must be in the same component
    # as the original element that would go to position i in the sorted array.
    
    # Find the position of each value in the original array
    value_to_positions = {}
    for i in range(n):
        val = a[i]
        if val not in value_to_positions:
            value_to_positions[val] = []
        value_to_positions[val].append(i)
    
    # Create the expected component for each position in sorted array
    # For each value in sorted_a, find its original positions and check components
    sorted_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        sorted_positions.append(sorted_a[i])
    
    # For each value in the sorted array, check if it can be placed at that position
    # by seeing if it belongs to the same component as the relevant original element
    
    # Actually, here's the key insight: if we can rearrange elements within components,

    # elements that will become sorted_a[i] in the final array must all belong to the same component.

    # So the answer is 1 if and only if for every value that appears in the sorted array,
    # all original positions that contain that value are in the same component (or actually,

    # Actually, simpler: we can form connected components. Elements can be rearranged

    # But since elements in the same component can be rearranged freely, we just need

    # 1. Find connected components of indices based on shared prime factors
    # 2. For each component, collect the values
    # 3. Sort the values within each component
    # 4. Place these sorted values back into the original positions of that component
    # 5. Check if the resulting array is sorted
    
    # But this is not correct either, as we can move values to any within-component position.
    
    # Actually, the sorted array is achievable if and only if for every component, 
    # the values in that component can be arranged in sorted order in their positions.
    # Since we can rearrange freely within components, we can always achieve the sorted order
    # within each component.
    
    # But this would mean the answer is always 1, which contradicts the example.
    
    # Let me check the example 2: [2, 9, 6, 5, 6, 7, 3]
    # Prime factors:
    # 2: {2}
    # 9: {3}
    # 6: {2,3}
    # 5: {5}
    # 6: {2,3}
    # 7: {7}
    # 3: {3}
    
    # Components:
    # 2: {2}
    # 9: {3}, shares with 6,3 -> 6,3,9
    # 5: {5} - isolated
    # 7: {7} - isolated
    # 6: already in component with 9,3
    # 6: already in component with 9,3
    
    # Components by index:
    # Component 0: 2 -> {2} 
    # Component 1: 9 -> {3}
    # Component 2: 6 -> {2,3}
    # Component 3: 5 -> {5}
    # Component 4: 6 -> {2,3}
    # Component 5: 7 -> {7}
    # Component 6: 3 -> {3}
    
    # Actually, indices 1,2,4,6 have prime factors involving 3:
    # 9:3, 6:2,3, 6:2,3, 3:3 -> so indices 1,2,4,6 are connected
    # Index 0 (2): {2}, no shared prime with anyone except the 6's which have 2
    # Index 3 (5): {5}, isolated
    # Index 5 (7): {7}, isolated
    
    # So components:
    # C0: index 0 (value 2)
    # C1: indices 1,2,4,6 (values 9,6,6,3)
    # C2: index 3 (value 5)
    # C3: index 5 (value 7)
    
    # Values in each component:
    # C0: [2]
    # C1: [9,6,6,3]
    # C2: [5]
    # C3: [7]
    
    # Can we sort these components? 
    # Sorted values in each component:
    # C0: [2]
    # C1: [3,6,6,9]
    # C2: [5]
    # C3: [7]
    
    # If we assign these to the positions in the component:
    # Position 0: should be 2 (original has 2)
    # Positions 1,2,4,6: should have 3,6,6,9
    # Position 3: should be 5
    # Position 5: should be 7
    
    # But in the original array, at position 1: 9, position 2: 6, position 4: 6, position 6: 3
    # In the sorted array, position 0: 2, position 1: 3, position 2: 5, position 3: 6, position 4: 6, position 5: 7, position 6: 9
    
    # The values don't match. For example, position 2 in sorted array has 5, but originally it had 6.
    # But 5 is in component C2 (index 3), not in component C1.
    
    # The key insight: for the array to be sortable, the values in the sorted array must
    # be such that each value appears in a component that contains its original occurrence.
    
    # But we can move values within components. So we can move the 6's from positions 2,4 to positions 3,4,
    # and the 3 from position 6 to position 1, and the 9 from position 1 to position 6.
    
    # However, the value 5 is in component C2 (index 3), so it can only move to positions in C2.
    # In the sorted array, 5 should be at position 2, but position 2 is in component C1.
    
    # Ah! This is the key. The value 5 can only move to positions that are in the same component as its original position.
    # Position 3 has value 5, and it's in component C2 (only index 3). So 5 can only go to position 3.
    # But in the sorted array, 5 should be at position 2 (since sorted order is: 2,3,5,6,6,7,9).
    
    # Therefore, the array cannot be sorted.
    
    # So the algorithm:
    # 1. Find connected components of indices based on shared prime factors
    # 2. For each component, find all positions in that component and the values at those positions
    # 3. In the sorted array, for each position, check if the value belongs to the same component as the original position
    #    - More precisely: for each value in the sorted array, it can only be placed in a position that is in the same component
    #      as the original position that has that value
    
    # Actually, we need to check: for each value in the sorted array, if there exists an original position
    # with that value that is in the same component as the target position.
    
    # But it's more direct: for each component, the values that appear in the sorted array within that component
    # must be exactly the values that originally appeared in that component.
    
    # But the component might contain values that don't appear in the sorted array.
    
    # Actually, the values don't change. The issue is the positions.
    
    # The correct condition: for the sorted array to be achievable, for each value v, all the positions
    # where v appears in the sorted array must be in components that contain at least one original occurrence of v.
    
    # No, that's not it.
    
    # The correct and simplest solution: 
    # For each index i, in the sorted array, the value sorted_a[i] must be in the same component as the original value a[i].
    
    # Let's verify with example 1:
    # [6,4,2,3,7] -> sorted [2,3,4,6,7]
    # Prime factors:
    # 6: {2,3}, 4: {2}, 2: {2}, 3: {3}, 7: {7}
    # Components: 
    # 6 and 4 share 2, so connected
    # 6 and 2 share 2, so connected
    # 3 and 6 share 3, so connected
    # 4 and 2 share 2, so connected
    # 7 is isolated
    # So components: {0,1,2,3} and {4}
    
    # For each original index:
    # i=0: a[0]=6, component 0-3. sorted_a[0]=2, which should be in component 0-3. 2 is in component 0-3 (position 2), good.
    # i=1: a[1]=4, component 0-3. sorted_a[1]=3, which is in component 0-3 (position 3), good.
    # i=2: a[2]=2, component 0-3. sorted_a[2]=4, which is in component 0-3 (position 1), good.
    # i=3: a[3]=3, component 0-3. sorted_a[3]=6, which is in component 0-3 (position 0), good.
    # i=4: a[4]=7, component 4. sorted_a[4]=7, which is in component 4, good.
    
    # So it works, answer is 1.
    
    # Example 2: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3] -> sorted [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
    # Components: 
    # 0: value 2 -> {2}
    # 1: value 9 -> {3}
    # 2: value 6 -> {2,3}
    # 3: value 5 -> {5}
    # 4: value 6 -> {2,3}
    # 5: value 7 -> {7}
    # 6: value 3 -> {3}
    # Connections:
    # 0 has {2}, 2 has {2,3}, 4 has {2,3}, 1 has {3}, 6 has {3} -> so 0,2,4,1,6 are connected
    # 3 has {5} -> isolated
    # 5 has {7} -> isolated
    # So components: C0 = {0,1,2,4,6}, C1 = {3}, C2 = {5}
    
    # Check for each original index i:
    # i=0: a[0]=2, component C0. sorted_a[0]=2. Position 0 is in C0, good.
    # i=1: a[1]=9, component C0. sorted_a[1]=3. Position 1 is in C0, good (3 is in C0).
    # i=2: a[2]=6, component C0. sorted_a[2]=5. Position 2 is in C0, but 5 is in component C1, not C0. Bad!
    # i=3: a[3]=5, component C1. sorted_a[3]=6. Position 3 is in C1, but 6 is in component C0, not C1. Bad!
    # i=4: a[4]=6, component C0. sorted_a[4]=6. Position 4 is in C0, good.
    # i=5: a[5]=7, component C2. sorted_a[5]=7. Position 5 is in C2, good.
    # i=6: a[6]=3, component C0. sorted_a[6]=9. Position 6 is in C0, good.
    
    # We have multiple bad checks. The answer is 0.
    
    # Therefore, the algorithm:
    # 1. For each number, find its prime factors
    # 2. Use union-find to group indices that share prime factors
    # 3. For each original index i, the value sorted_a[i] must be in the same component as the original index i
    
    # How to verify that a value v is in the same component as index i?
    # We need to find the component of index i, and then check if the value v appears in any original position
    # within that component.
    
    # But actually, we don't need to find where v appears. We just need to know if the value v is attainable
    # in the component of i.
    
    # For value v, it can only be in positions that are in the same component as the original position where v appeared.
    
    # But we need to check if value v can be placed at position i.
    
    # The value v must have appeared in the original array at some position j such that j and i are in the same component.
    
    # For each value v, we need to know which positions in the array originally had v.
    
    # Algorithm:
    # 1. Build components (using union-find)
    # 2. Create a dictionary: value -> list of indices where it appears
    # 3. For each position i in the sorted array, check if sorted_a[i] can be placed at i:
    #    - Find the component of i (component_id of index i)
    #    - Check if there exists an original position j such that:
    #        a. a[j] == sorted_a[i]
    #        b. j has the same component as i
    # 4. If all positions satisfy this, answer is 1, else 0
    
    # This should work.
    
    # Implementation:
    
    # Get prime factors for each number
    def get_prime_factors(x):
        factors = set()
        d = 2
        while d * d <= x:
            while x % d == 0:
                factors.add(d)
                x //= d
            d += 1
        if x > 1:
            factors.add(x)
        return factors
    
    # Build components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Get prime factors for each position
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Connect indices that share prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection not empty
                union(i, j)
    
    # Build value-to-indices mapping
    value_to_positions = {}
    for i in range(n):
        val = a[i]
        if val not in value_to_positions:
            value_to_positions[val] = []
        value_to_positions[val].append(i)
    
    # Get component of each index
    comp_of_index = list()
    for i in range(n):
        comp_of_index.append(find(i))
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Check for each position in sorted array
    for i in range(n):
        target_value = sorted_a[i]
        current_comp = comp_of_index[i]  # component of position i
        
        # Check if there exists an original position j where a[j] == target_value
        # and j is in the same component as i
        found = False
        if target_value in value_to_positions:
            for j in value_to_positions[target_value]:
                if find(j) == current_comp:
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if not found:
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$

Output

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1

Output

0
