Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is a classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Chicken McNugget problem" - finding the largest number that cannot be expressed as a non-negative integer combination of given numbers.

Key observations:
1. If we have two numbers that are coprime (gcd = 1), then there exists a largest number that cannot be expressed as their sum
2. If the GCD of all numbers is greater than 1, then all numbers that can be formed will be multiples of that GCD, so there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
3. If the GCD of all numbers is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed (the Frobenius number)

## Mathematical Background

For two coprime numbers a and b, the largest number that cannot be expressed as ax + by (where x,y ≥ 0) is ab - a - b.

For more than two numbers, if they have GCD = 1, then the Frobenius number exists and can be found using dynamic programming.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find GCD of all numbers**: If GCD > 1, return -1 (infinite numbers cannot be formed)
2. **If GCD = 1**: Use the Frobenius number algorithm with DP
3. **Edge cases**: Empty list or single number

## Algorithm

For the general case with multiple numbers having GCD = 1:
- Use dynamic programming to find all numbers that can be formed
- The largest number that cannot be formed is the answer

## Implementation

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    from math import gcd
    from functools import reduce
    
    total_gcd = reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if total_gcd > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming approach
    
    # Sort numbers to make the DP more efficient
    zombies.sort()
    
    # Maximum possible value to check
    # We can use the fact that if we can form a consecutive sequence of length 
    # equal to the smallest number, then we can form all numbers beyond that
    
    max_zombie = max(zombies)
    
    # Use DP to find which numbers can be formed
    # Set dp[i] = True if i can be formed
    # We only need to check up to max_zombie * max_zombie (this is a safe upper bound)
    # But we can be more efficient - if we can form max_zombie consecutive numbers,
    # then we can form all numbers beyond that
    
    # We'll check up to max_zombie * max_zombie as a safe bound
    bound = max_zombie * max_zombie
    
    # If we have only one number, then we can only form multiples of that number
    if len(zombies) == 1:
        # For a single number, all numbers that are not multiple of that number cannot be formed
        # So the largest such number is infinity, but we need to check if GCD is 1
        # Since GCD is 1, we need to find the largest non-multiple
        # Actually, if GCD=1 and only one number, that's impossible since GCD would be that number
        # So this case shouldn't happen with GCD=1
        return -1
    
    # DP array: dp[i] = True if i can be formed by sum of zombies
    dp = [False] * (bound + 1)
    dp[0] = True  # 0 can always be formed (sum of zero elements)
    
    # For each number from 1 to bound, check if it can be formed
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        for zombie in zombies:
            if i >= zombie and dp[i - zombie]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We know that once we have a consecutive sequence of max_zombie numbers that can be formed,
    # then all larger numbers can be formed (by adding the smallest number)
    
    # Find the first position where we have max_zombie consecutive True values
    # Then the answer is the last position before this consecutive sequence
    
    consecutive_count = 0
    last_consecutive_start = 0
    
    for i in range(bound + 1):
        if dp[i]:
            consecutive_count += 1
            if consecutive_count == max_zombie:
                # All numbers from i - max_zombie + 1 onwards can be formed
                # So the largest unformable number is i - max_zombie
                return i - max_zombie
        else:
            consecutive_count = 0
    
    # If we reach here, it means the consecutive sequence wasn't found
    # This should not happen with GCD=1 and at least 2 elements
    # So we just return the last unformable number
    for i in range(bound, -1, -1):
        if not dp[i]:
            return i
    
    return 0
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the edge case and the algorithm. Let me revise:

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    from math import gcd
    from functools import reduce
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    total_gcd = reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if total_gcd > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming
    
    # Special case: if only one number, GCD would be that number
    # But we already handled GCD > 1, so only one number case with GCD = 1 is impossible
    
    # Sort for efficiency
    zombies.sort()
    max_zombie = zombies[0]  # smallest number
    
    # We need to find the Frobenius number
    # If we can form a consecutive sequence of length equal to max_zombie,
    # then we can form all numbers beyond that point
    
    # We'll use DP up to a reasonable bound
    # The bound can be max_zombie * max_zombie (though this might be too large)
    
    # Actually, we can be more efficient - the Frobenius number for multiple numbers
    # is generally less than the product, but we need to ensure we find it
    
    bound = 10000  # reasonable upper bound for the problem constraints
    
    # dp[i] = True if i can be expressed as sum of zombies
    dp = [False] * (bound + 1)
    dp[0] = True
    
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        for z in zombies:
            if i >= z and dp[i - z]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Once we have a run of max_zombie consecutive numbers that can be formed,
    # then all larger numbers can be formed (by adding the smallest number)
    
    # Find the first position where we have max_zombie consecutive True values
    consecutive = 0
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        if dp[i]:
            consecutive += 1
            if consecutive == max_zombie:
                # From (i - max_zombie + 1) onwards, all numbers can be formed
                return i - max_zombie
        else:
            consecutive = 0
    
    # If we reach here, there's no such point, so find the last unformable number
    # This shouldn't happen with GCD = 1 and multiple numbers
    for i in range(bound, -1, -1):
        if not dp[i]:
            return i
    
    return 0
```

View Extracted Code 
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    from math import gcd
    from functools import reduce
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    total_gcd = reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if total_gcd > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming
    
    # Special case: if only one number, GCD would be that number
    # But we already handled GCD > 1, so only one number case with GCD = 1 is impossible
    
    # Sort for efficiency
    zombies.sort()
    max_zombie = zombies[0]  # smallest number
    
    # We need to find the Frobenius number
    # If we can form a consecutive sequence of length equal to max_zombie,
    # then we can form all numbers beyond that point
    
    # We'll use DP up to a reasonable bound
    # The bound can be max_zombie * max_zombie (though this might be too large)
    
    # Actually, we can be more efficient - the Frobenius number for multiple numbers
    # is generally less than the product, but we need to ensure we find it
    
    bound = 10000  # reasonable upper bound for the problem constraints
    
    # dp[i] = True if i can be expressed as sum of zombies
    dp = [False] * (bound + 1)
    dp[0] = True
    
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        for z in zombies:
            if i >= z and dp[i - z]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Once we have a run of max_zombie consecutive numbers that can be formed,
    # then all larger numbers can be formed (by adding the smallest number)
    
    # Find the first position where we have max_zombie consecutive True values
    consecutive = 0
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        if dp[i]:
            consecutive += 1
            if consecutive == max_zombie:
                # From (i - max_zombie + 1) onwards, all numbers can be formed
                return i - max_zombie
        else:
            consecutive = 0
    
    # If we reach here, there's no such point, so find the last unformable number
    # This shouldn't happen with GCD = 1 and multiple numbers
    for i in range(bound, -1, -1):
        if not dp[i]:
            return i
    
    return 0

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is a classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Chicken McNugget problem" - finding the largest number that cannot be expressed as a non-negative integer combination of given numbers.

Key insights:
1. If the GCD (greatest common divisor) of all numbers in the list is greater than 1, then all numbers that are multiples of this GCD can be formed, and there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed (specifically, all numbers not divisible by the GCD).
2. If the GCD is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed (this is the Frobenius number).

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Check if GCD > 1
- If GCD of all numbers > 1, then any number that is not divisible by GCD cannot be formed
- Since there are infinitely many numbers not divisible by GCD, the answer is -1

### Step 2: If GCD = 1
- When GCD = 1, there exists a largest number that cannot be formed
- For two numbers a and b where GCD(a,b) = 1, the Frobenius number is a*b - a - b
- For more than two numbers, there's no simple formula, but we can use the fact that if we have a set of numbers with GCD 1, then all sufficiently large numbers can be formed
- We can use the "coin problem" algorithm: start from 1 and check each number until we find a consecutive sequence of length equal to the maximum number in our input

### Step 3: Algorithm for GCD = 1 case
- Start from 1 and check each number to see if it can be formed
- Use dynamic programming: if a number can be formed, mark it as True
- Keep track of the largest number that cannot be formed
- When we find k consecutive numbers that can be formed (where k is the maximum element in our list), then all numbers beyond this point can be formed
- The answer is the largest number that cannot be formed

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    import math
    from functools import reduce
    
    # Calculate GCD of all numbers
    gcd_all = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then infinitely many numbers cannot be formed
    if gcd_all > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, we need to find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # This is the Frobenius number for the given set
    
    # For the general case with GCD = 1, we can use the fact that
    # once we have a consecutive sequence of length = max(zombies), 
    # all larger numbers can be formed by adding the maximum element
    
    max_zombie = max(zombies)
    
    # Use dynamic programming to find which numbers can be formed
    # We only need to check up to max_zombie * max_zombie (practical bound)
    # In practice, we check until we find max_zombie consecutive numbers that can be formed
    
    # We'll check up to max_zombie * max_zombie to be safe
    max_check = max_zombie * max_zombie
    
    # dp[i] = True if i can be formed by summing the numbers
    dp = [False] * (max_check + 1)
    dp[0] = True  # 0 can always be formed (sum of zero numbers)
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for num in range(1, max_check + 1):
        for zombie in zombies:
            if num >= zombie and dp[num - zombie]:
                dp[num] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We know that once we have a consecutive sequence of length = max_zombie,
    # all numbers beyond that can be formed
    largest_non_formable = 0
    for i in range(1, max_check + 1):
        if not dp[i]:
            largest_non_formable = i
    
    # Check if we found a finite answer
    # If there's a gap in the consecutive sequence of length max_zombie,
    # then the answer is the last number that can't be formed
    consecutive_count = 0
    for i in range(max_check, 0, -1):
        if dp[i]:
            consecutive_count += 1
            if consecutive_count == max_zombie:
                # All numbers beyond this point can be formed
                break
    
    # The largest non-formable number is the last one we found that is not formable
    # But we already computed this in the loop above
    return largest_non_formable
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the algorithm. The consecutive sequence idea is correct, but my implementation might not be efficient enough.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is the classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Chicken McNugget problem". We need to find the largest number that **cannot** be expressed as a sum of given positive integers, where each integer can be used any number of times.

Key insights:
- If we have numbers that are coprime (gcd = 1), then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed, and it's called the Frobenius number.
- For two numbers a and b with gcd(a,b) = 1, the Frobenius number is a×b - a - b.
- For more than two numbers, there's no simple formula, but there's a known result: if the GCD of all numbers is greater than 1, then there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed (all numbers not divisible by the GCD).
- If the GCD of all numbers is 1, then there is a finite largest number that cannot be formed.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Check if GCD of all numbers is 1**:
   - If GCD > 1, then infinitely many numbers cannot be formed → return -1
   - If GCD = 1, then there is a finite largest number that cannot be formed

2. **For GCD = 1**: Use the dynamic programming approach to find the largest number that cannot be formed

3. **Algorithm**:
   - Use a boolean array to track which numbers can be formed
   - For each number in the input list, mark all possible sums as formable
   - Find the largest number that cannot be formed

Let me implement this:

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    import math
    from functools import reduce
    
    gcd_all = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then infinitely many numbers cannot be formed
    if gcd_all > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, we need to find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming approach
    # We need to find the largest number that cannot be expressed as sum of given numbers
    
    # We'll use the fact that if the GCD is 1, then all sufficiently large numbers can be formed
    # We can find the Frobenius number for 2 numbers, but for more, we need to check
    
    # Set a reasonable upper bound - if the numbers are up to 1000, we can go beyond 10000
    # The largest number that cannot be formed is bounded by the Frobenius number + some margin
    
    # For practical purposes, we can use the bound: max(zombies) * max(zombies) 
    # But a safer bound is max(zombies) * max(zombies) - min(zombies) - max(zombies)
    
    # Actually, we can use a simpler approach: check numbers sequentially until we find a sequence of consecutive numbers
    # Once we have k consecutive numbers that can be formed, all larger numbers can be formed
    
    max_zombie = max(zombies)
    # We'll check up to max_zombie * max_zombie as an upper bound
    # This should be safe for the problem constraints
    
    # Use DP to mark formable numbers
    # We need to find the largest number that is NOT formable
    formable = [False] * (max_zombie * max_zombie + 1)
    formable[0] = True  # 0 can always be formed
    
    # Fill the DP table
    for num in zombies:
        for i in range(num, len(formable)):
            formable[i] = formable[i] or formable[i - num]
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(len(formable)):
        if not formable[i]:
            result = i
    
    # Check if we found any such number
    # If all numbers from some point onward are formable, we can stop
    # But we need the largest that cannot be formed
    
    # Actually, we should find the largest gap
    # Since GCD = 1, there is a finite largest non-formable number
    # Our DP should find it
    
    return result
```

- We need to find that number (should be 5)

- 3: formable (3)

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    import math
    from functools import reduce
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    gcd_all = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then infinitely many numbers cannot be formed
    if gcd_all > 1:
        return -1
    
    # For GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be formed
    max_zombie = max(zombies)
    
    # Use dynamic programming to find which numbers can be formed
    # We need a sufficiently large upper bound
    # Since GCD = 1, all sufficiently large numbers can be formed
    # A safe upper bound is max_zombie * max_zombie (or max_zombie * max_zombie - something)
    
    # We'll use max_zombie * max_zombie as the bound
    bound = max_zombie * max_zombie
    
    formable = [False] * (bound + 1)
    formable[0] = True  # 0 can be formed
    
    # Mark all possible sums
    for num in zombies:
        for i in range(num, bound + 1):
            formable[i] = formable[i] or formable[i - num]
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    # We need to find the maximum i where formable[i] is False
    for i in range(bound + 1):
        if not formable[i]:
            result = i
    
    # Actually, since GCD = 1, there is a finite maximum
    # But we might have found a result that's not the largest?
    # Our loop finds the largest i where formable[i] is False
    
    return result
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is a classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Chicken McNugget problem". We need to find the largest number that **cannot** be expressed as a sum of given positive integers, where each number can be used unlimited times.

Key insights:
- If the greatest common divisor (GCD) of all numbers is greater than 1, then only multiples of that GCD can be formed, meaning there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed.
- If the GCD is 1, then there exists a maximum number that cannot be formed (this is the Frobenius number).

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Check if GCD > 1
- If GCD of all numbers > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
- So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed → return -1

### Step 2: If GCD = 1 (Frobenius problem)
- For two numbers a and b with GCD = 1, the Frobenius number is `a*b - a - b`
- For more than two numbers, there's no general formula, but we can use the fact that if GCD = 1, then beyond a certain point, all numbers can be formed
- We can use a dynamic programming approach to find the largest number that cannot be formed

### Step 3: Dynamic Programming Approach
- Use a boolean array to track which numbers can be formed
- For each number, try all possible combinations
- Find the largest number that cannot be formed

## Implementation

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    from math import gcd
    import functools
    
    g = functools.reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if g > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, we need to find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming to find all possible sums
    max_num = max(zombies)
    
    # We need to find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # The Frobenius number for multiple variables has no simple formula
    # So we'll use DP to find the answer
    
    # Since the numbers can be used unlimited times, we can use DP
    # We need to find the largest number that cannot be formed
    
    # The answer will be at most max(zombies) * max(zombies) in theory
    # But we can use a more efficient bound
    
    # We'll use the fact that if a number can be formed, then all larger numbers
    # that are greater than or equal to some value can also be formed
    
    # Let's use DP with a reasonable upper bound
    # For two coprime numbers a,b, the answer is a*b - a - b
    # For more numbers, the answer is less than or equal to the Frobenius number for two numbers
    
    # We'll use a DP approach with upper bound of max(zombies) * max(zombies)
    # But we can optimize by knowing that once we have a contiguous sequence of length = min(zombies)
    # of numbers that can be formed, then all larger numbers can be formed
    
    # Let's set our bound to max(zombies) * max(zombies) * 2 as a safe bound
    bound = max(zombies) * max(zombies) * 2
    
    # DP array: dp[i] = True if i can be formed
    dp = [False] * (bound + 1)
    dp[0] = True  # 0 can be formed (by taking no numbers)
    
    # For each possible sum
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        for z in zombies:
            if i >= z and dp[i - z]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We need to find the largest i such that dp[i] is False
    result = 0
    for i in range(bound + 1):
        if not dp[i]:
            result = i
    
    # If no such number exists (all numbers can be formed), return 0
    # This shouldn't happen with GCD = 1, but just in case
    return result if result > 0 else 0
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the problem. The bound might be too large and inefficient.

Let me optimize this using a better approach:

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    from math import gcd
    import functools
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    g = functools.reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if g > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming with a reasonable bound
    
    # The Frobenius number for multiple coprime integers is at most
    # the product of the two largest numbers minus their sum
    # But for simplicity, we'll use DP with a bound of max(zombies)*max(zombies)
    
    max_zombie = max(zombies)
    bound = max_zombie * max_zombie
    
    # DP array: can form i?
    dp = [False] * (bound + 1)
    dp[0] = True
    
    # Fill the dp array
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        for z in zombies:
            if i >= z and dp[i - z]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(bound + 1):
        if not dp[i]:
            result = i
    
    return result
```

def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    from math import gcd
    import functools
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    g = functools.reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if g > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use dynamic programming with a reasonable bound
    
    # The Frobenius number for multiple coprime integers is at most
    # the product of the two largest numbers minus their sum
    # But for simplicity, we'll use DP with a bound of max(zombies)*max(zombies)
    
    max_zombie = max(zombies)
    bound = max_zombie * max_zombie
    
    # DP array: can form i?
    dp = [False] * (bound + 1)
    dp[0] = True
    
    # Fill the dp array
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        for z in zombies:
            if i >= z and dp[i - z]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(bound + 1):
        if not dp[i]:
            result = i
    
    return result
